put it near a page.** Nobody answers, and the reason nobody answers is the chapter's argument: **the three lines the instrument said at the copper seam on Wednesday are about a bead and what a bead holds and what a man is going to keep; they were never about a hand; a bead goes against a wetted lot and cannot be laid against a sheet of paper at all; the question in this room has been a hand on a page for four days; there is no instrument in this district that will read a page, and anybody who goes to Cray and buys one for that counting house will get a device that reports a bean.** Dunmar writes none of it down, and says why when Lissa asks: *a book that puts a bead's rules in beside a page about a hand is a book that has told a district clerk the wrong thing about both of them.* **Lissa Vale refuses to be asked, and refuses in the wrong direction, and says it before anybody in the room has time to want it and after she has had about an hour to want it herself** — *you are not going to be asked, by anybody in this building, and if a district officer asks you in a room in a year you will say the same thing and I will have said it first and in my own name and not in yours. A name in a book costs this building: the clean record on the eighth of last month, three acres on the sixteenth, and a woman her day rate in a market she is not in.* She does not know what the next thing will be and has decided to be the one who says so before the yard works it out. **It is not Sera Quill's silence, it is not Lissa's fourth refusal, it is not numbered, and nobody asked her.** And the last beat costs the wrong book: the reader asks for the **variety ledger** — a Crown form's demand, from a district office, made to a third party — and finds **three names she has never seen in any book anywhere and that are not in the printed index either, and can say nothing is wrong with them, and says that if anybody wanted to buy a line out of that book they could not tell.** She will not say whether the March's four struck lines are the same four. **Lissa Vale writes the three names on a sheet of seed-house paper, enters them against the ledger and not in it with the hour, and puts the sheet on the middle of the table beside the fieldbook and the use log, and neither of them asked the other for anything.** Tova Reed does not go to her case: *next week, and I am not going to be able to tell you tonight whether I am keeping the map or giving it away, and I would rather tell you that than decide in front of a stranger.*

---

# VOLUME 02 — BATCH 0002, CHAPTERS 60–68, MOVEMENT 2: THE MARKET

**Nine chapters, season days 65–84, 25,388 words by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings, after the review pass; the draft stood at 25,041.** Movement 2. **Days 61 onward are in the month after the month after October, so this batch runs from the fifth to the twenty-fourth of one month, and October is two months back and is named nowhere. Day 85 is unused and is the next batch's. Sixteen uses of *two months back* were applied to days 31–60 in the draft and all sixteen are corrected; the phrase now appears three times and all three are genuine October.** The weekday chain is anchored on day 61 = the first = Thursday, locked in Volume 01 Ch 49. **The batch prompt's own day-clock table is off by one against its own weekday column and the chapters are dated from the weekday chain; the full correction, every distance on the page, and the March's tin reconciliation are in `state/continuity.md` under *VOLUME 02 — BATCH 0002*.**

**60. The Market Does Not Notice** (days 65–67, the fifth to the seventh, Mon–Wed, the seed-house table, the outer field, the low corner, ~3,080)
The Ashcombe winter issue arrives at the fen as the Fennmere post's own copy, left on the table by a carter who had time because the rain stopped, and the number on the wrapper is **a hundred and forty sheets to seed houses for this winter**, and the print carries **four hundred names bought by nine hundred smallholders**, and the Ashcombe black oat is in it in its column under its printed name. **Dunmar Fitch enters the refusal in the form he uses and it is the batch in one line: *the refusal was entered on the twenty-eighth of last month, the sum not received, a shilling a line for every line that names a variety, the number of houses the refusal reached is one.*** Tova Reed's own arithmetic gets worse rather than better: forty-one lines is forty-one shillings, two pounds and a shilling, and one smallholder in twenty buying a printed line is forty-five sales a line, and there is no third place for the lines to be. **And the afternoon turns on a question Dunmar asks in the flat voice he uses for procedure — what does the winter issue cost. Two shillings. The body buys the enemy's printer, takes under a minute, and puts the two hundred pages on the middle of the table beside the fieldbook and the use log, and they stay there for the rest of the batch, and Lissa Vale says the true thing: *two shillings a season would be nothing; two shillings is a subscription.*** The ledger is then checked against the index line by line and **the three names Osvalde Bream could not call wrong are not in the printed issue at all**, and the refusal stands between a book nobody copies and a book anybody may. The ordinary work does not stop: the leak at five-eighths twice measured at the seventh hour, the pit with four feet in it and not up, nineteen acres standing, the Longneck leaning eleven degrees, the toe ditch holding, the monthly root-change report filed as nine lines that say they looked and could not say what changed. **Ends on Tova Reed at the counting-house end with form C/4 in front of her and the date nobody can check: the fen is not the addressee, has never seen a copy of the notice and is not entitled to one, the district counts the day of service, and the last day of the fourteen is the fourteenth of this month, nine days off — and the March's answer has to be written this week by a woman nine hundred miles away who does not have the volume's three acres in her.**

**61. A Trade Article** (days 68–69, the eighth and ninth, Thu–Fri, the seed-house table, the Fennmere yard, ~2,860)
**Coll Renner brings a printed quarterly himself on a Thursday morning and says the only thing about himself first: I did not write it, I did not send it, I did not know it was coming, and I have been up since the fourth and I cannot find out who did.** The sheet is two pages stitched, with the mark of the professional body on the front — **the same body Pike's Volume 01 circular went out in, the same roll that carries Renner's license and nine other men's in this district** — a district and a year, a title that says nothing about a fen, and under a plain heading **a description of a result**: a wetted lot laid against a living bead cut from grafted stock reports the ground it was grown on in four seconds without a clerk, with figures for a lot of one variety, **and no name and no license number and no yard and no district and no holder.** Tova Reed finds it accurate on the first read and says that is the problem. **Renner's four things: I did not write it; I cannot stop it; it is technically correct; and a technically correct description of a device that makes a seed lie is the most dangerous thing anybody has ever printed about this holding. Four licensed men in Tarrow including him, and the body examines on a working and not on a paper, and a man who has watched him do it four times in a yard has the whole of it.** He also supplies the volume's structural sentence, unasked: **what is printed in a quarterly of a professional body is not a raid, it is the trade — it goes where the trade goes, it costs what the trade charges, every act of it is correct and timely and structural, and if it ruins a seed house in this fen that is a cost and not a crime. Do not let a later room confuse the two, and do not let me be the man who taught you to.** Dunmar notices it is the same sentence the district uses and neither of them has a right to be amused. He has told his foreman and will tell his nine on Friday, and **if somebody offers him money for a collar by the middle of next month he will come up the fen road and say so in writing.** Gael Senn's finding is the chapter's: **the book is the only defense anybody has, and a book only works if somebody reads it, and their bead is in a tin and anybody may buy one.** At Fennmere he gives up his own finding — not this fen's book, which does not describe the method, and not the woman with the list of eleven holdings; **it came from somebody who sat where he sat.** The figures are then checked against this holding's own trials of the thirtieth of last month and **no fault is found, which is itself entered as a finding, because a false article could be answered and a true one can only be lived with.** Ends on a letter from the Thursday post, from **a seed house in a town two hundred miles off with no fen in it, addressed to a counting house in Cray, asking whether the Ashcombe black oat is the one in the index.** Dunmar asks the procedural question; Tova answers that the trust answers, the book says the oat came in an unlabelled tin eleven years ago and is not the oat, both things stand, and **we do not decide it for him, and if he decides wrong he loses a shilling, which is what a buyer loses, and it is not our business and we may not make it ours.**

**62. Nobody Is Going To Walk It** (days 70–71, the tenth and eleventh, Sat–Sun, the gatepost, the embankment, the seed-house table, ~2,740)
**Corvin Slee comes four miles to stand at the posts on a Saturday and will not come in, and the district clerk with the satchel asks Marek to walk the line of the standing iron as this holding finds it, to set beside the district's, and Marek says no in front of him and gives a reason that is the chapter: *a holding that is owed a survey cannot also be the body that goes and collects it, because the collecting is the part that makes it theirs. If I walk it, it becomes my walk. If I write it, it becomes my book. I will not make their cost into my leverage.*** Slee does not forgive the damage and does not have to, and says the fen is at least not taking the map. **The clerk tells him a refusal in writing is still a refusal in writing and can be read against the holding later by a man in an office who has never stood at a gatepost, and Marek says that is correct and wants it entered anyway.** The collie's report of the nineteenth, two months back, is in three places and nothing has been written underneath it and nobody is going to interpret it. **Then the argument the chapter needed: the woman with the shilling asks Marek, in front of the yard, to walk the line himself, because it costs him an hour and eleven of the forty-one would drink one day longer — and she says she has voted for the open book and would keep her half of it either way — and Marek refuses, and the reason is the engineer's: *if I take their chalk and put it in my book, then in four years a man in a room opens two books and one of them is mine, and he weighs mine, because mine is the engineer of record.*** Ilsa Kern says nothing, which in that yard is a position. **And then Slee comes back down the road at the seventh hour and puts the copy of the low quarter's own survey into Marek's hand, and Marek refuses that too: a copy in this building is a copy this building can be asked for, and then it is a person's to lose, and an office in Fennmere is a better keeper than a person.** The copy sits unopened on the middle of the table and is entered as received and unopened with a line under it saying the sheet is not of this holding and is not in this holding's book. **The cost is entered on Dunmar's own initiative: the low quarter keeps its map, this holding keeps its hands off it, and it costs the fen the only leverage it has in the district on the one ground where forty-one houses are still drinking off a bed it killed — and a later room will want to know whether we knew that at the time, and we did.** **The ninth section-nine note, dated the tenth**, and it carries the closing sentence. On the eleventh the district form arrives and asks this holding to produce its own map of the low quarter, and the answer, signed by Marek **against the interest of the application**, is that it has never been asked to and that it would not be a map if it were.

**63. Kellan Rusk** (days 72–74, the twelfth to the fourteenth, Mon–Wed, the seed-house table, the well house, the outer field, the gate, ~3,100)
**Kellan Rusk, forty-three, a well contractor holding a monitored license on the Tarrow water record — a different roll from the rootwright roll, which has three names and carries Coll Renner's license, and he is not on it — comes up the fen road alone with the district's monthly return in his coat and puts three correct figures on the middle of the table before he says anything else: his own side draw never above three hundred a day for six years from a log open to any member at any hour, the head as the log claims it at eleven hundred a day, and the ground as the ground took it at seven hundred and twenty, which is the fen's own figure and which he reads back from the fen's own page to show he has read it. Eighty a day unaccounted for for six years, one hundred and seventy-five thousand two hundred.** He says it with an edge, and **nobody in that room likes him less afterwards and everybody likes him less because of how he says it**, and Anneke Roos asks him to say it again without the edge and he says he has no other way. Then the sentence that is the batch's third pressure: **the three together, with the standing pressure log, the seed annex and the use log, are enough to prove this holding's own draw against itself, which is the last thing any of them wants, and it is true, and he is saying it to help, and he knows what help costs in this yard.** The return is produced, read front and back, **matches the first figure with no fault in it — which means anybody who asks can now get at it.** What he wants is not money and not a reduction: **to be asked at the next examination, in front of a district officer, to say what he said on a bench in the mud with the figure in his hand, the name in the box being an office and not a man.** It is minuted as asked for, word for word, and **the yard does not agree to it in the room.** **The batch's one Fieldbook panel is spent here, at the eleventh hour, at the well house, on the head.** Lissa Vale writes the use-log line first and reads it back, Kellan tests the contact with a plain wire gauge and writes the test down first, the instrument comes out of the case on Marek's mother's table where it has been since the twenty-first of last month, and it says three plain lines: **This is the mouth. / There is more than one source and it will not say which. / A node does not keep a list.** It costs eleven seconds he cannot account for, four inches of forearm hot and staying hot, and a hand that will not work for two days. **Lissa Vale writes nothing further and gives the reason in one sentence — there is nothing in it that a man with a rule can check, and a line in a book that cannot be checked is a line somebody can use — and the reading is entered as a contact and not a finding.** On the thirteenth Gael Senn checks the three figures against the standing pressure log with two rules and finds no fault, and the use-log lines touching the head are read aloud so that the figures sit beside four lines of record. **On the fourteenth the C/4's fourteen days run out, and the fen enters the date as a computation and not as a fact, because it is not the addressee and is not entitled to a copy of the notice and cannot learn today whether the notice has been answered.** Ends at the gate on the evening of the fourteenth with Kellan's line, entered verbatim because a sentence that good does not get paraphrased by a clerk in a cold room: **you have built a book that anybody can read and you have put everything in it that is true about you, and a book like that is not evidence. It is an invitation.**

**64. The Order Of The Work** (days 75–76, the fifteenth and sixteenth, Thu–Fri, the seed-house table, the north annex, the gate, ~2,740)
**Coll Renner comes a week early and says so at the gate rather than letting the yard work it out: he has had the form in his coat since the thirtieth, drafted and not sent, and then a description of what he did in this yard went to four hundred doors and he found out on Friday what four hundred doors means.** The form is **RW/3, one page: a box for the licensed engineer with his name and district already in his own hand, a box for the four conditions — anchor, path, return, witness — printed and empty, and a line at the foot for the order of the work, blank, which is the only line on the sheet anybody wanted.** It is not a contract, it is a schedule of works, and his foreman could carry it on a Tuesday to a holding he has never seen. **Marek's hand goes to the line and does not fill it in**, and the reason is Chapter 57's said again to a different man: *a list of things that have been done is a list of things anybody can do, and eleven fen holdings cannot afford the compost; write the order down and somebody will follow it and starve a field correctly — they will do it in the right order and it will still fail for want of a twentieth.* Dunmar studies the boxes with a clerk's eye and finds it a good form, and says a good form refused is still refused. **And the third of the three conditions in clause three comes out in the open: the district has not printed what it is and is not required to describe a condition in advance of the examination at which it is tested, so this holding is under a clause it cannot read and cannot comply with — and Renner's answer is that every holder in this district has one and every one of us is frightened of the same blank.** Then the price list and route, unasked, in his own hand on two sheets of yard paper: **day work four shillings a day, a yard a pound a month, a copper collar two shillings, a crew of four for a season along eleven named farms at eighteen pounds, compost at four shillings with the shortfall shown and not hidden.** It is the first material anybody has put on that table in a season. **Tova Reed makes him read every figure aloud and then refuses it, and the refusal is about the compost and not about the man** — *a route that needs four hundred hundredweight a month at four shillings is not a route for this district, whatever the day rate says; nine men cannot make compost out of a price list.* **She keeps the two sheets and does not give them back, and says the compost and not the other thing, and the other thing is the four lines she has never indexed, and it is entered nowhere and stays a thing two people knew.** Bread and cheese at midday with the figures still on the table; the box counted empty, the tin unmoved at fifty-six days, the Longneck standing. On the sixteenth Renner walks the yard, asks three professional questions and writes none of the answers down, and puts a hand flat on the earth over the graft and leaves it there too long. Ends at the gate: **the form will be back in a season with the line at the foot filled in by somebody else, that is what he is paid for, if it is not yours it will be somebody's and it will be a worse one made by a man who has never watched two people make a graft — and I do not know whether that is a threat or a market, and I would like it entered that I said so and could not tell from where I was standing.**

**65. The Seed Buyer Writes Back** (days 77–78, the seventeenth and eighteenth, Sat–Sun, the seed-house table, the well house step, ~2,505)
**The Ashcombe sheet goes to press on the sixteenth without anybody in the fen knowing and comes on the Sunday post of the eighteenth with the trust's letter behind it: one variety, source disputed, printed name not established, no holder named and no district named, the doubt in the same type as the names.** A seed house two hundred miles off in a town with no fen in it reads it and does not buy the line; his letter is addressed to the counting house in Cray with a second copy to this fen because the fen holds the ledger, and he will take forty bags at a time when the name is established and would rather hear from the seed house than from the index. **The line is the March's, the March's holding is the trust's, the trust is a named party to a charter condition, and the refusal of a shilling a line has cost the March a sale of forty-five bags.** Tova Reed enters that in her own hand in the trust's book and the fen's, in daylight, at the middle of the table, with the hour, and Dunmar reads it back twice **because it is the first line she has ever entered that says she was wrong about a thing and not about a number**, and she asks for it not to be softened by anybody including her. **The yard wants to call it an attack — the man of about fifty first and loudly, Ilsa Kern second with a bar in her hand — and Dunmar will not let the word stand and makes Marek put the proposal and the refusal on the page together with Marek's name against the proposal: a buyer who reads that a name is not established and does not buy is doing what Osvalde Bream did on the fourth of last month; a district that prints a category cannot be caught at anything, because the category is true; a printer who prints a doubt cannot be caught at anything, because the doubt is true; the oat is doubtful and this holding has said so itself for eleven years in two books, and if a doubt is a wrong then eleven years of this holding is a wrong.** Dunmar reads the doubt, then the letter, then the eighth line, to the five households who came, and the man of about seventy asks for his initial on nothing. The forty-five bags are written in pencil beside the entry and struck out, because a number of bags that were not sold is not a fact about a book, and Dunmar makes her initial the striking. **Then the second discovery: the buyer's second copy carries a line at the foot saying his house would wait until the fourteenth for an answer before taking the question to his own printer — and the fourteenth was four days ago, and the trust is four days past the district's date on a notice the fen forwarded, and the fen cannot write to Cray to ask, because the moment this holding writes about a notice it was not served with is the moment it is a party to a notice it was never given.** Lissa Vale writes the answer in four lines in her own hand and reads it back twice, and it is the truth: **the seed house does not know, it has never known, it copied its own name off a Crown list in a year it does not name, and that is the whole of what it knows.** Ends: the woman who asked the question is a buyer and she is owed an answer by the fourteenth, and the fourteenth was four days gone before a word of it was written, and it goes on the Thursday post of the twenty-second, four lines long, and late.

**66. What The Book Is For** (days 79–80, the nineteenth and twentieth, Mon–Tue, the yard, the well house step, the seed-house table, ~2,750)
**The batch's midpoint, and it is a defeat. On the nineteenth Dunmar Fitch reads the whole of it out, once, in public, in the yard, in daylight, from the middle of the table — the standing pressure log from the first page with the hours on every line including the morning's, the ninth logging of the beat and the tenth entered that morning in the same hand, the use log in Lissa Vale's voice after him, the seed annex with its eight lines against the trust's interest, the bed at three and a half degrees, the compost at nothing put in, the tin and the days it has been at risk, the 399 hundredweight at seventy-nine pounds sixteen, the three acres as a decision and not a charge, the nine margin notes, the certified copy in a Fennmere file, the copy going to Cray, the three figures a well contractor put on the table on the twelfth, and a district clerk's own note of a refusal made at a gatepost on the tenth. Five households came and two strangers on the road stopped to listen and nobody told them to go, and it took the better part of an hour and nobody left.** **The reading is interrupted twice by the strangers, and the second interruption is the proof that the argument is real: one man makes Dunmar say out loud, to a stranger, that he looked for nine days for a reason the third levy came back at the same sum with the same fourteen names and did not find one — *that is the first time anybody has said that to me* — and the other says there is a name on the notes, the engineer's, that he had heard before and had not heard one of them say out loud, and Dunmar says *there is not going to be*. Neither is entered.** Then the sentence that turns the batch: **a book that anybody may read is worth exactly what a reader is prepared to do about it, and this holding has spent a season building the first and has not found the second.** **And the mechanism, said by Anneke Roos: the body cannot repeal it, because a body cannot consent to the removal of its own record, because the removal is the consent — clause two on the fourth sheet, put there to protect the book against the district and now protecting it against us, and it works perfectly, and she initialled it against a sentence about the book being evidence and would like it read back that the sentence she initialled has turned out to be a sentence about the book being a thing that cannot be got back.** The man of about fifty objects in the correct terms — he has been the loudest about the beat and about the levy that came back the same twice, both are in that book, and a man with forty sheep does not have to have been in a yard to find out what this holding has been doing to its own water — and Marek answers with his own three acres as a decision and not a charge, and with the sentence that a thing only the six of them read is not a record, it is a diary. **Tova Reed's door is the last thing said before the vote: the trust is entitled to a copy of every entry, so the vote of the fourth, two months back, is a vote about who may stand in a yard and not about who may hold a copy — and she is not going to go to Cray to warn them, because a steward who warns people about the size of their own rights is a steward who has decided the rights are too big.** The body votes on the twentieth, standing, in daylight, **five hands, two of them from households that did not vote on the fourth and one of them saying so out loud first, and the two strangers entered as not voting because a book anybody might read has to show who decided and who only heard.** It is not a repeal. **The tenth section-nine note, dated the twentieth**, carrying the closing sentence, with Dunmar's own line under it recording that the closing sentence is off the sixth and the eighth because both were records and not arguments, so that the chain cannot be seen to drop a link.

**67. The Answer** (days 81–83, the twenty-first to the twenty-third, Wed–Fri, the gate, the seed-house table, the well house step, ~2,670)
**A woman of about forty comes to the gate on the twenty-first with a pencil and a notebook and no standing whatsoever, and says in the first minute that the seed house two hundred miles off that wrote to Cray about the Ashcombe oat is her house, and that she has come two hundred miles to buy an answer about a bean, and that the fifth sheet says any member, any stranger and any clerk may read without notice, and that on the twentieth the body voted that the reader is a person and a person is anybody who comes.** Lissa Vale comes out to the gate and reads it herself, **and says why out loud: I wrote the line that says anybody may ask to have an entry read, and a rule somebody else keeps is a rule that gets kept the way a form gets kept.** She reads the whole of the fen's hydrology — all four use-log lines with the lateness declared, the leak at five-eighths with the second rule the one that reads smaller, the tenth beat beside the ninth with cause not established on both and nothing under either, the 399 hundredweight, the bed, the tin at sixty-two days — **and nothing is withheld and nothing is softened, and the stranger writes in pencil the whole way through with the notebook open on the table so that Dunmar can watch it, which no one in that yard had thought to do.** She stops Lissa twice on figures, and asks the question the fen had not been asked: **what the third of the three conditions in clause three is**, and is told nobody knows and it is in the book and out of the building now. She asks the reader to say plainly that her own reason for being there is printed in a book two hundred miles off, and says out loud, to be entered, that **she is going to write to a printer about it, that her house buys on print, and that nobody in this yard can stop her and nobody in this yard wants to, and she is saying it so that it is in the book too** — and then sets out in advance what the letter will say, which is that a body in this district keeps a book any stranger may ask to have read, and a woman in it read the whole of it aloud at a table to a buyer with a pencil, and everything she read was worse for them than anything they had been told, and they were four days late with an answer they could have written in a morning. **Marek warns her, before she writes, that his name is in the book and that her printer will see a name and a number and that will be the whole of what he has, and she writes it down anyway, and Lissa Vale writes on the back of her own page, and tells Marek afterward and not in front of her, that he told her the cost before she asked for it.** Midday bread on the well house step, and two things a reader does that the fen has not seen before: **she asks what the closing sentence of the margin notes means and works out for herself that the notes lacking it are the sixth and the eighth and that both say underneath that they were records and not arguments; and she says out loud that whoever wrote the ninth note was arguing with himself in public, and that she has never in twenty-two years of buying seed seen a book do that, and that she is going to think about it and is not going to put it in her letter.** Ends on the twenty-third, on her way out, with the question nobody in the yard has asked a stranger in six weeks — is there a use log for the fieldbook — and Lissa Vale's one word, **no**, and the stranger writing it down; and Lissa's own closing judgment to nobody in particular: being readable is a position and not a virtue, a woman came two hundred miles about a bean and was handed a season's water, no one asked this holding to be a good citizen, it is not thanked for it and cannot be, and the next one will come asking for the thing in the use log and not the thing under it, and this holding has never once written a line underneath anything and is not going to start because a woman from a town with no fen in it has got a pencil.

**68. The First Reason** (days 84–85, the twenty-fourth and the unused twenty-fifth, Sat–Sun, the seed-house table, the yard, ~2,605)
**The yard agrees on the twenty-third, without a vote and without a speech, eleven days after Kellan Rusk's request was minuted, and the district writes back in four lines: a return under a monitored charter is a document, and a document is produced before a figure is heard. The examination is on a Saturday, because districts hold sittings on Saturdays.** The officer who comes on the twenty-fourth **is not Hester Carrow** — her authority ran to the end of the year and she has a tray with three other districts in it — and she is **Hilary Skeath of the district water office for the Tarrow district, about thirty-five, who gives her name once at the gate and is entered in the book by the clerk, because a name said once at a gate is a name that belongs in a book and not in a memory.** She asks for the return before she will hear the figure. He produces it. **It does not contain the figure.** And what breaks is not the evidence: **it is that his return to the district is a document, and the figure is in the fen's book and not in his own, which is a breach of a condition of his own charter — disclosed by him, in that room, on the day he asked to be asked, before anybody had found it.** **Nobody rescues him, nobody forgives him, nobody apologizes to him, and Lissa Vale does not look away from him, which is the most anybody gives him; the breach goes into the fen's book in his own words beside the figure and not below it, and into the district's file in the officer's words.** Her ruling: **a person who discloses a breach of his own condition can still be believed about the thing that is not the breach** — the same rule that held for a sealed envelope in this district once before. **And then the thing that made the day worth a season, done correctly, and unwanted by the fen: she cannot act on the figure, and she has a file, and the file is the only place in this district where the draw of a private charter can be recorded without anybody's consent, and she enters eighty a day for six years on the strength of his own disclosure — and it will be read the next time anything in this district comes up for a renewal or a variation or an objection. *That is what your open book did. Not to you. It is not a favor and it is not a trap and it was not a clever thing anybody did.*** Dunmar enters it in her words and **she asks to read the line before it goes, which is the first time in a season a district officer has asked to read a line in this holding's book before it was entered.** Then she asks the question he has waited a season for — is there anything this holding wants the district to require of it — **and he asks, aloud, in front of a district officer, the question nobody in the yard has asked in a season, which is what the fen wanted the district to ask him: will the district require the full-draw load test that the engineer of record declined to perform, and will the district order it, and he offers to perform it himself on the district's order and to take the paper for it and says he will not have the first copy.** She says yes, and says why unasked: **there is a clause in this charter with a condition in it that neither the district nor this holding can test, and a node with an untestable condition in it is a node she has to defend at every renewal, and a number she can check is worth more to this holding than any objection she could raise.** It costs the fen nothing and gives it the only thing it has wanted since the twenty-first of last month. Then she closes the file, all but the string, and says the last thing: **the district will want the map next, because a Crown seed-route map of this district is nine years old and there is a legend mark on it that this holding can now name. Nobody in that room said the word.** Tova Reed does not touch her case; Marek does not look at the sheet lying on the table since the twenty-ninth of last month under a reader's own marginal line in her own hand; Kellan goes out into the yard without waiting to be spoken to. **The charter expires on the twenty-ninth of the month after next, thirty-five days from this morning, and nobody in that yard had said it out loud in three weeks, and the only thing on the paper that is still a fact about the future is a line at the foot of a form in an office nine miles off.** **The eleventh section-nine note, dated the twenty-fourth, the longest single line this holding has ever entered**, carrying the closing sentence, because the note carries the map question and the map question is an argument.


---

## The Volume 02 Batch 0002 review pass — what these summaries now say differently

**`reviews/volume-02-batch-0002.findings.md` holds the twenty-two findings; `state/continuity.md` § *The Volume 02 Batch 0002 review pass* holds the state-side record. Five things in the summaries above are now wrong and are corrected in place, and a later writer should not read the uncorrected version of any of them.**

1. **The batch is days 65–84 and Ch 68's card is 84–85 with only the twenty-fourth used.** Day 85 is Chapter 69's.
2. **Ch 63: Kellan is on the water record and not the rootwright roll**, and the use-log line Ch 63 reads aloud is **all six lines**, with the thirtieth of last month and the twelfth named. The twelfth's line records the contact and the hour and **nothing else**, so the panel is still in no document.
3. **Ch 60: the winter issue is paid for on the Friday, in a hand to a carter into Fennmere, and arrives on the seventh** — the order precedes the purchase, which the draft had reversed. **Cray is nine hundred miles by the March road and a hundred and eleven further by the ordinary cart, the distance from the seventh to the fourteenth is seven days, and the fourteenth is now stated on the page as a computation from an assumed service date.** The three acres went sour three weeks ago. The index is a hundred and forty copies of two hundred pages.
4. **Ch 62: no mender has been in the fen since the fifth of last month** — day 35, Ch 35, and the bible said the sixteenth. **Ch 62's evening line covers the leak only and adds no pulse entry, so the tenth logging of the beat is entered once, on the nineteenth, in Ch 66.**
5. **The buyer is a woman throughout** (Chs 61, 65, 66, 67), **Ch 64's eighteen pounds is four levies and not a year's raise, Ch 66's levy hunt has run since the twenty-fourth of last month and not for nine days, and the stranger's lateness is eight days on the twenty-second and not four.** Ch 66's public reading and Ch 67's reading to the stranger both now read **all six lines of the use log**, and the man of about fifty's non-vote is entered in Ch 66's vote.

**Staging is a known weakness and is not a summary error: eight of nine chapters sit at the seed-house table, and the review's two proposed relocations were declined and recorded rather than made.**

---

# VOLUME 02 — BATCH 0003, CHAPTERS 69–76, MOVEMENT 3: THE MEMORY IN THE SEED

**Eight chapters, season days 85–104, 23,108 words by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings.** Movement 3. **The batch runs from the twenty-fifth of one month through the fourteenth of the next; day 92 falls inside Ch 72 and carries no chapter and no entry. The weekday chain is anchored on day 61 = the first = Thursday. The charter expires on day 119, thirty-four days from this batch's first day and fifteen from its last. The March's tin is day minus nineteen throughout and stands at eighty-five days at risk on the last day. No month is named anywhere in the batch. Staging was varied deliberately: Ch 71 is at the well house and the pit with no reading at the middle table at all, and Ch 72 is on the seed-house floor at night with thirty-one trays down.**

**69. Four Seconds** (days 85–87, the twenty-fifth to the twenty-seventh, Sun–Tue, the gate, the seed-house table, ~2,680)
**The batch's reversal, and it is a success.** A cart from Fennmere on the twenty-fifth brings a **copper collar with a bead of living rootwood in it, sold and not made** — Renner's own second note: *I did not make this. I am selling one I cut in the spring and I have four left and I am not selling the four. If you want one to go up that road faster than a Tuesday cart, that is money, and I will say no, and I would rather say no in writing on a Sunday than in a yard on a Wednesday.* **Lissa Vale pays two shillings out of a seed house tin that held eleven and reads the amount out loud**, and says the nine that are left are the float for a month in which no lamp oil will be bought. **She says the collar is not the instrument and a wet cloth is not a contact, and then the room writes a use-log line for one anyway, three days later, and nobody remarks on it.** Tova Reed brings four lots and sets them in an order she explains so nobody can say she chose one. **The fen's own bean reads as this fen and is correct and useless, and the yard's first discovery is that the device cannot tell a field from a holding.** The March's wet-line rye from the flowerpot reads as the March and is the control. **The grafted seedling from the same pot does not read a place at all**: it gives an order, not a ground — the thirtieth of last month, the fifth hour, the north annex, a cut out of a pot and a cut out of the field bed, a tie of green withy, and a decision to keep one of two and throw the other away. **Lissa Vale identifies it as the ninth tray, the one with a black bend in the stem, kept against the drill because it was the worst of the forty-one and the only one with a clean root, and says the reason is not a matter for a collar. She refuses the reading, in nine lines, in her own words, and the clerk enters the refusal with her name on it and not the thing's — the first thing this holding has declined to enter since the book was opened.** What goes in the book is the fact of the test, the hour, the number of lots, and the two results that were places. Tova Reed's sentence: **it does not report a place, it reports the last hand, and a hand is a person, and a person is the only thing in this trade that has never been for sale.** Ends with the fourth lot named — the last of the March's dry-land rye, four winters of a line that has failed every year, kept in a tin in a counting house nine hundred miles away — **and a date, the third of next month at the ninth hour, said out loud because she has found out twice this month what a date costs.**

**70. A Certificate** (days 88–90, the twenty-eighth to the thirtieth, Wed–Fri, the gate, the seed-house table, ~3,120)
**Ottoline Fray, of Fray and Daughters at Marrowfield, forty-one, four generations, forty-one smallholders, twenty-two years, says her name and her house at the gate before she is asked for either**, and the clerk enters both and reads them back to her. She brings her own collar, bought in the spring from a man she names and whose price is not entered, and a sheet of printed paper: **a certificate of origin, two hundred sent out with her winter list and thirty-one used, four shillings a lot, three ruled lines with printed heads and no others — *ground*, *season*, *keeper of record*.** She reads a Marrowfield lot in four seconds and it is a place. **And then Tova Reed asks whose hand is in the keeper line, and the whole of the volume is in the answer: the device cannot tell anybody a person's name — four hundred lots read and never once a name — so the keeper line is the one line no device in the world can fill in, and the person who fills it in is the person who sells the lot.** The ground and the season are true, the keeper is a man she chose, and the two facts sit on the same sheet in the same ink. Then the lot's own record: **eleven parts carrier, one part line.** Every grain is descended from a cutting of the March's dry line, the certificate does not say so, there is no column for it, and a man who sows it gets the line for one season and the carrier for every season after, and nothing in the trade will tell him which he has. **"I have not sold anybody a lie. I have sold them one part in eleven and told them what the one part is."** The offer: **a shilling a line on ninety-one lines, two pounds a month at the quarter, term eleven years, this holding named as keeper of record on every certificate and forbidden afterward to say the seed was certified by anybody else** — and the buyer's own reason for the term: *at the end of it I hold your history and you hold a promise and the promise is the cheaper of the two things.* **The fen cannot answer for the trust in four days: three days to Cray and three back if the road holds, and the March road has been out at two places since the fourteenth.** And the buyer's answer to being told so, said at a table and entered afterward: **she will not take the book on the second, or on the fourteenth, or on the twenty-ninth, or at all, and will not go round a body that could not answer her in four days, because the last time she took a line from a house she could not telephone it went into nine hundred smallholders in a season and she did not find out for two years.**

**71. Eleven Hundred** (day 91, the first of the next month, Sat, the well house, the pit, the gate, ~2,760)
**The first of the month is a Saturday, the district holds sittings on Saturdays, and the load test the district ordered is performed.** A clerk with a form case and **Coll Renner as the licensed engineer**; the order is the district's paper over Hilary Skeath's name; the paper is the district's and the holding is not to have a copy, and Marek's own undertaking of the twenty-fourth not to have the first copy goes unused because the district did not want it. **The eleventh tray of the Longneck is moved off the bench so a man can read a dial.** Gael Senn takes her morning line first, at the seventh hour, because the order of the two is not a thing anybody may choose: **the leak at five-eighths, twice measured, second rule reading smaller, into the housing and not the pit, and the pit not up.** Then the **eleventh logging of the beat** — nine in the quarter hour, one run of four, at the rail, cause not established, **licensed person present one, which it had never been** — and nothing underneath it about what a licensed man eight feet away means. The monthly report goes up as nine lines. **The test: eleven hundred from the ninth hour, held one hour and ten minutes, and the ten extra minutes entered in the fen's own book before the figure because a test longer than the order is a different test.** The head holds and the frame hums from about ten hundred and forty and the Longneck comes up eleven degrees and the bed is not fed for the hour and ten. **And at the ninth hour and forty minutes, with the pump at eleven hundred, Gael Senn goes into the pit and the leak reads five-eighths, twice, second rule smaller — the same figure it has carried for thirteen days, measured under a load nobody in this district has ever applied to it. It did not move and nobody wrote underneath it and it is not mended.** The arithmetic goes on the page beside the figure and **the test accounts for nothing of the eighty a day, in Marek's own words: a test at the head measures the head, a number about a mouth and not a number about a place.** Then the hinge: **the third of the three conditions in clause three is put on the table at the examination and is still not described**, and the answer is a form's three lines — the third condition is tested at an examination, no condition is tested twice in the same term, the term ends with the charter — **so the examination at which it is tested is the first one held after the term ends, and the man who holds the node on that day will be told on a form, in a room, with no season's notice.** The clerk asks to go home and is not refused, and that is entered. Renner at the gate: he is worth it to nobody in the fen and would rather the record and the trade were in the same county than the same quarterly.

**72. The Third Time** (days 93–94, the third and fourth, Mon–Tue, the seed-house floor at night, ~2,580)
**Tova Reed chooses the day, the hour, and the floor.** Thirty-one trays and nine tins come down off three shelves, the lamp goes on the floor on a flat box, the fieldbook on a chair, the tables against the wall, and **Lissa Vale reads out what is in each thing as she sets it down in an order nobody else in the room can reproduce.** The man of about fifty is allowed to sit down and is told not to get used to it. **The chapter's contrast is two lots of the same bean out of the same sack, sown the same day: the one out of the bulk reads as a place, and the one out of the ninth tray reads a hand and a season and a decision, and the third thing taken off the bench was thrown and where it went is in it as well.** Of thirty-one trays, **six read as a place and those six are the six that came out of a bulk sack and had not been chosen by anybody since; the other twenty-five read as a keeper.** Anneke Roos makes the class be named on the page before the word *seedkeeper* is written over it: *a lot that has never been chosen by anybody reports where it was grown, and a lot that has been chosen by anybody reports who chose it and what they chose, and the second one is a better answer and it is not a better fact.* Then the case is unlocked. **The March's dry-land rye, four winters, reads a place — the low dry above the third sluice, the sowing before last, a ley — and it is correct, and Tova Reed says nothing and looks at it and does not move.** She then takes out a sheet with no mark on it: **eleven holdings, the eleventh month two seasons back, the year the dry line failed, cuttings of the March's dry line, the eleventh of them this fen, no column for cuttings in the trust's book, in no book at all, carried in a case for two seasons**, and gives her reason, which is that it is the only thing she has ever done that she could not put in a column, and says she would do it again, and asks the room to write it down, **and nobody writes it down, and Lissa Vale declines to read it back, and that refusal is the answer.** The sentence the volume turns on: **the line is not lost, it was never lost, it is in eleven places and it is in no book, a man in a shed at Marrowfield has a cutting of it and sold a lot of it and gave a certificate and the certificate is true, and she has written *the dry-land line is still lost* in three books including a copy that went to Cray, and it is a wrong and it is hers and it is the largest wrong she has entered in eleven years.** The ninth line goes in on the fourth, in daylight, in her own hand, in the trust's book as well and on the Thursday post, unsoftened, and the four lines never indexed go in with it, and one of the four is the Ashcombe oat. At the end she tells Marek to say in the yard that **a certificate says a name and a ledger says what happened, and hers does both** — and that she will deny she said it at this table.

**73. What It Is Worth** (days 95–96, the fifth and sixth, Wed–Thu, the seed-house table, the gate, ~3,095)
**Renner comes up the road on the fifth with no tools, no form and no collar, and says at the gate that he has been asked three times since the eighth of last month to cut a bead for money and has said no three times and none of them took offense, and that he has come early because somebody has been in his yard asking his foreman what a collar weighs.** Then the money, on four sheets, read aloud twice and once more by the clerk. **Two pounds a month is eight shillings, compost is four shillings a hundredweight, so the offer buys two hundredweight of the three hundred and ninety-nine short, which is five hundredths of the gap, which is nothing** — and Tova Reed's sentence: *it is not a price, it is a lease on the only thing in this fen that cannot be grown.* The man of about fifty argues for it and his argument is entered with his name and the hour; the woman with the shilling says eight shillings a month is eight shillings a month; **Jonas Byre argues that the day the first certificate goes out the levy comes round at four times what it is because the men will know there is money somewhere, and says he is sorry to say it and means it; Ilsa Kern says nothing, which in that yard is a position.** **And Marek argues for it, and asks for it to be minuted in his own bad words, and says the room should not let him say it twice because a man who says a thing twice has stopped being a witness and become an advocate.** Then Tova Reed's alternative: **two hundred pages, the true name of every line with the wrong one beside it, the four never-indexed lines, her ninth line, and on the front page that the seed house copied one of its own names off a Crown list in a year it does not name; a shilling a copy to anybody.** Eleven shillings, out of the trust's printing account at a shilling a page without reference, which is the account and not her discretion, **and the number of copies she thought might sell in a year is penciled under it and struck out, with the clerk initialling the striking.** **The batch's one Fieldbook panel is spent here, at the eleventh hour, with Renner fitting his own clip, the contact tested with a plain wire gauge and the test written down first, the use-log line written by Lissa Vale and read back before anything, and the reading not entered in any document:** *The ring reads the last hand. / It does not read the ground, and it never will. / There is nothing here a buyer may not buy.* It costs eleven seconds, a hot forearm and a day of the hand. **Renner tells the room what entering them would cost him — any man with a wet cloth could read them off a collar in a market and sell nine carriers for every line and he could never say where it was published — and asks that they not be entered, and the fen decides the batch's central question without the one instrument that might have settled it.**

**74. In Front Of The Book** (days 97–99, the seventh to the ninth, Fri–Sun, the yard, the well house step, the gate, ~2,730)
**The batch's climax, and the decision is taken in the yard in front of the book with the buyer at the gate**, on the clerk's ruling, because a body that takes a decision a buyer has paid for in a yard has already lost it. The two things are read out in full before anybody speaks, and then **the thing that decided it is not an argument: Tova Reed asks for the fourth of the four never-indexed lines to be read, and Lissa Vale reads it**, in a slow careful voice, not looking at her, not stopping, not softening a word — **the Ashcombe oat, entered eleven years ago by her, at Cray, in the eleventh month, out of a tin with no label on it, not able to be established, and an unestablished name is not sown, and thirty-one households would otherwise have gone into the winter without a bean.** Tova Reed asks for two things: that it was a decision about food and not about a name, and that her name is now going to be on every copy of the printing in this district. **And Marek Vale says the sentence he was asked to say, badly, with the number in it, and before the counting: the largest wrong in the eleven years of this book was entered by the person standing next to me, not by a printing office four counties away and not by a man in a shed at Marrowfield, and a vote taken with that in the room is a different vote from one taken without it.** The vote: **seven hands could be counted and seven were in the yard; five for the printing and against the certificate; two against; and one of the two was the engineer of record, who put his own name against his own vote.** Lissa Vale's hand went up first and she would not say which way, and refused to state it, and gave the reason: *the last thing a reader does is tell a room what a page means before the room has read it* — and nobody thanked her and nobody said a word to her about the ninth tray. **Anneke Roos then does her job and finds one thing wrong after the vote, and she is right: eleven shillings out of the trust's account on a standing order, in a month when the trust has not been asked, on a road out at two places — and Tova Reed has the answer written out already.** And then she says in the yard, while losing, that two hundredweight a month is five hundredths of the gap and that the gap will be three hundred and ninety-nine hundredweight on the first of next month and the month after, and that none of it is going to change because they chose the truth, and that it is the only good trade anybody has put in front of that body in a season and it does not pay for the compost. At the gate, **Ottoline Fray refuses the certificate at any price and buys four copies at a shilling and puts them in her winter list as a buyer's reference**, says she has been in the trade twenty-two years and could not take them to the first of a hundred and forty houses that could show where its mother's seed came from, and asks the fen to teach her a way of asking. **The twelfth section-nine note, dated the seventh, twenty-two days to the expiry.**

**75. The Man Who Refused** (days 100–101, the tenth and eleventh, Mon–Tue, the gate, the seed-house table, the well house door, ~3,060)
**Coll Renner comes up the road on the tenth and hands Marek the offer at the gate before he says anything, so that nobody can say he formed his opinion first — and the clerk enters that he came five days before the fifteenth he promised, and not that he forgot.** The offer: **twelve pounds, his quarter, for a collar of a stated size and bead, and a standing instruction that collars be cut on a Sunday, when no holding is holding an examination and no licensed man is on a road.** Refused in writing on the ninth, copy kept, letter sent back by the hand that brought it. **His reason is the fen's own spring rule that no work on this node is done between dusk and dawn without a licensed engineer standing in it: a man who makes a bead on a Sunday is a man whose beads have no record, and if I did it there would not be a book in this fen afterwards that could do one thing about it, and I would have taken twelve pounds for it, and the book would have been right.** Then the part that costs him: **there were two men, one of whom buys on the Ashcombe index, and Renner knows his name and will not say it in a book, and gives the reason that is the only part of it that belongs to this fen — if I say his name in your book, then your book is a thing somebody can take off me** — and **Anneke Roos initialled, faster than any in a season, a standing of this holding: a man may decline to name a buyer in a book this body keeps, the clerk enters that he declined and why, the body does not ask again and does not enter the name if it is told one**, and the clerk added that nobody in the yard is to go and find it out. **Five collars are accounted for: four made in eleven years, two given, one sold, one lost — the one in this yard — and a fifth man in this district with one of unknown origin, entered so that nobody can say afterward that it was not.** Then he asks for the last page of the fen's printing, and is argued into two sentences under his two hundred words, **one of which is that a lot of one part in eleven is a sale the law allows and the trade has agreed to and no office in this district will call it a fraud, and that is a notice and not an opinion.** He admits he filled in his own form RW/3 on the sixth and sent it to a holding on the Marrowfield road that cannot afford a cooperative, and told the fen first because the fen refused him and he did not have to. **And the fen tries two things on the eleventh and both fail: the district writes back in four correct lines that a licensed man's private arrangement is not a matter for the district, there is no form, and the Assembly will examine a man and will not say what it found; and Jonas Byre walks nine miles to two of the eleven holdings on Renner's price list and one shuts the door after thanking him and one does not open it at all.**

**76. The Price Of It** (days 102–104, the twelfth to the fourteenth, Wed–Fri, the seed-house table, the gate, the yard, ~3,060)
**The C/4 answer comes down the March road on the twelfth, twenty-eight days after the last day of the fourteen this holding worked out for itself and could not check**, in a canvas bag with a seal broken and mended twice: nine sheets produced late and in full with a schedule of four things the trust cannot produce and the reason in each case, and **one of the four is a column** — the trust's histories have eleven printed columns, the seventh is *source*, it has never held anything but a place, and there is no column for cuttings, none for a steward, none for a line dispersed and not returned from. **The trust has entered its own fault and says so: a line kept in one book is a line that can be got at, a line kept in eleven grounds and no book is a line that can be sold, that cannot be corrected by paying anybody, and can only be corrected by saying it out loud in a book that anybody may read.** The March road is out at two places and the trust cannot move a crate until the season turns and has asked whether the fen will hold its seed for a season, and the answer is yes and carriage cannot be paid. **The cutting list is now in two books in two countries with no private copy, and the eleventh name is a party to a charter condition, and the fen can now be asked for it.** The district writes for the map on the thirteenth and it goes back on the Thursday cart **with a page and a half of the fen's own that says the branch is used for the supply of eleven households and is a local holder in fact, makes no claim to the Crown's layer, and notes that the ninth legend mark is a handover to whoever is not the holder and that this holding is the holder and was not on the sheet when the sheet was printed nine years ago and nobody appears to have been required to say anything about it** — and Tova Reed's last line on the return is that somebody in an office is now going to have to decide what to be done about a holder that appeared, and that the question was opened on the twenty-fourth of last month by a district officer who did not open it. Forty copies off the press; Lissa Vale reads all two hundred pages out in the yard; one sold in the yard, one to Marrowfield. **The arithmetic does not go anywhere: nothing put in for the second month running, 399 hundredweight short, the bed at three and a half degrees, the tin at eighty-five days unmoved, the leak at five-eighths unmoved under a full draw and not mended, the beat logged eleven times, the graft fed about a twentieth, the charter fifteen days from ending, and Ruben Ashe named in this holding's own book as returning at that date.** The thirteenth note. And then, on the afternoon post of the fourteenth, on foot, by a woman who walked eleven days: **three lines, her name and no house, eleven households on ground the district says it maintains and does not, a shilling a copy to anybody who has one to sell, and the road out at two places** — and a boy goes down the fen road with the letter and a copy and a shilling of the fen's money, past forty copies on the middle of the holding's own table, on a road with no form, no charter condition and no license in it, while the lamp burns on its living bulb because somebody has to feed it tonight.

---

# VOLUME 02 — BATCH 0004, CHAPTERS 77–88, MOVEMENT 4: THE BRIDGE

**Twelve chapters, season days 105–129, 33,202 words by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings.** Movement 4. **The batch runs from the fifteenth of one month through the ninth of the next, crosses a month boundary after Chapter 84, and the charter expires at midnight on day 119, which is Chapter 84 and is the batch's midpoint. The weekday chain is anchored on day 104 = the fourteenth = a Friday and on day 61 = the first = a Thursday. The March's tin is day minus nineteen throughout and stands at 110 days at risk on the last day. No month is named anywhere in the batch and no document in it carries one. Staging was moved deliberately: Ch 79 is at the Fennmere counter, Ch 81 is on a road, Ch 83 is at Wrenlow and is the first scene in Volume 02 off this fen's ground, and Ch 85's examination is at the well house with the report going up on a cart.**

**77. Fifteen Days** (days 105–106, the fifteenth and sixteenth, Sat–Sun, the gate, the yard, ~2,985)
**The batch opens on the date Coll Renner promised and on a question the fen cannot answer.** He has nothing to report, which is the report, and he came because a carter told him a boy went down the fen road on the fourteenth with a book and a letter and a shilling — **and the carter came into his yard on the Friday, the fourteenth itself, and not on the Wednesday, which the first draft said and which put a report two days before the thing it reported; the fourteenth is a Friday** — and because a man who walks eleven days to ask for a record is not asking for a record. **In the yard, without being asked and before anybody has explained anything, he names the capacity in the trade's own words: a local conduit takes water from one place to another place; a seed-route bridge takes seed from one keeper to another keeper, and it has two ends and a return, and the return is the part nobody ever wants to pay for. And you do not name a bridge for the distance — you name it for the load, and a man who has written down the miles has not decided what the thing is for.** He reads the number cut into the board over the compost bay — thirty-nine days empty — and says he would like it to be a figure somebody can put on a form. **Jonas Byre asks the question the bridge puts into the room: who is the other keeper, and says it twice so that it is in the book twice, and the answer is that nobody in this yard has ever been there.** Tova Reed reads the return line aloud (the man of about fifty reads it back): **nothing put in, none available, four hundred hundredweight specified, three hundred and ninety-nine short, seventy-nine pounds sixteen, second month running.** She strikes her own line about a licensed man's sentence being a finding and initials the striking, the third time in a season. Ilsa Kern puts her hand on the empty bay and asks that its emptiness go in the book and be read out, **because in four months somebody is going to put a crate under it and there is no reason on this earth for them to know it was ever empty.** Ends on **the trust's question from the twelfth of last month and the arithmetic of holding somebody else's seed on a bed at three and a half degrees with no compost behind it**: ten trays where the bed holds twenty-one and the fen's own sowing is twenty-one, and Marek Vale refusing to make the decision about whose seed is on the ground by himself.

**78. The Schedule** (days 107–108, the seventeenth and eighteenth, Mon–Tue, the seed-house table, the well house door, the bay, ~2,775)
**A public schedule with two end columns and a return column, on a form that does not exist until somebody makes it, and it has no number on it.** Anneke Roos: a form with a number is a form somebody can get a second one of, and a copy has to be copied honestly or it is a forgery and not a document. Seven columns: the day, the crate, the lot and its keeper in the keeper's words, the weight, the first end, the second end, the return. **Renner's sentence is entered as his with the hour and is not put on the form, and the clerk enters that as a downgrade, because a licensed man's professional opinion in a book anybody may copy has no form in this district either.** The second end's column has no name in it and holds *eleven households, keeper not named*. Nailed up on the well house door on the seventeenth with four nails. **The first person to read a seed-route schedule aloud to a body of this kind is Jonas Byre, and his objection is correct: the seventh column is a debt, and a column anybody can read and that cannot be kept is worse than no column, and the people who read that door have been to it before and every form this holding has ever put on it has been correct.** He is not asking for the column taken off; he is asking for it read empty, out loud, and for the number of the readings written under it, **because a column that has never been filled is not a broken promise, it is a line anybody may write on.** The woman with the shilling asks who is going to fill it and says she would rather it was nailed up empty than not at all and does not know how those two go together. Ilsa Kern wants her own board taken off the bay and a second smaller board put up instead, and it is. **The man of about fifty does not object and gives the worst reason in the batch: he has wanted a person to come to this yard since the fourth of a month three months back and a book is a thing you can be read out of and a schedule is a thing you can arrive at, and he cannot tell who the schedule is for and has not asked.** **The fourteenth note.**

**79. A Place The Map Does Not Have** (days 109–110, the nineteenth and twentieth, Wed–Thu, the seed-house table, the Fennmere counter, ~2,670)
**A Crown act discovers a settlement by asking a question about it, correctly, in four lines**, because somebody wrote to this fen and a letter is a record and the district keeps records. **Tova Reed says it out loud and does not soften it: I sent them a holder and they have gone looking for what the holder is holding**, and asks for the correct part to be entered first. The reason is the counter-mark of the fourteenth of last month, and the district cannot leave a holder in a file. **At the Fennmere counter Perrin Osse explains, and laughs once: the form has a box headed with a printed head of six digits, and ground the district carries in schedule is ground with no holding number, so the form could not be filled in by her own office and a form with a box that cannot be filled in is a piece of paper with a hole in it. So she sent a letter instead — her first in eleven years to a body that has never given her any trouble — and she says plainly that a woman with no house would have had a form with a hole in it and no address to send it to.** The fen drafts its answer twice: the first attempt describes a settlement out of a woman's three-line letter and a figure off a trade article, and **Anneke Roos refuses the whole of it in four words — a survey of a place we have never been to, made out of a letter, with nothing in it a man with a rule can check.** What goes is the correspondence, the letter, the fact of the shilling, the statement that the fen has never been there and holds no map, and the reason a name is not on the form. Then **form S/10 arrives with the six-digit box, and Marek Vale writes in the box the words he used eleven days before about a different office and a different ground: this holding has never been asked to, and it would not be a map if it were — and under it, a second line: a sentence used twice is not a sentence any more, it is a practice, and a practice is a thing an office can make a form of.**

**80. The Terms Of A Bridge** (days 111–112, the twenty-first and twenty-second, Fri–Sat, the seed house, the yard, the well house door, ~2,760)
**Marek Vale drafts six clauses alone in a day and says at dusk that it is the best piece of drafting he has ever done and that he has been angry about it for an hour.** Anneke Roos finds nothing to fault in five and says she wants that entered, and that clause five — either end may stop the route on a day's notice by writing it in the book, no reason required, none to be entered — is the best sentence anybody has written in this building since the charter went up on that door, and will be annoyed if she is thanked. **The reading is in the yard in daylight with the book shut, and Tova Reed takes out her pencil and strikes clause two in one line: a copy of the record is kept at each end and each end answers on it.** Her reason: it makes her a party, a body that keeps a copy and answers can be examined about the document by anybody with the document, and there is nothing in the clause saying the examination has to be fair or at a counter or on a form; and there is no holding number for her ground, so a Crown form about her cannot be completed and the letter about her would go to a body that has a number, which is this holding. **Marek objects, in his own register, for the engineer's reason: a term with no respondent is a habit, and if in the middle of the winter the fen puts something in the seventh column it has not got, she will be the first to know and she has no document and nobody to go to, and this holding will be right.** Then he puts the harder half down himself: **clause four, which she is not striking, makes her a party to every line in the one book, and is the price of the document, and clause two was the attempt not to pay it.** She keeps clause four: *I would rather be examinable than quiet.* **Neither of them can answer it for the other and both of them are right, and that is entered as two competent people who looked at the same clause.** The terms go out with no enforceable term at the far end at all, and that fact is entered against the fen's own interest, in daylight, at the well house door, with the hour, and Jonas Byre reads it all back. Ends on the list of what she has done and **she is not thanked for any of it, and there is no clause in a charter or a fifth sheet or a book about a woman being thanked.**

**81. The Road** (days 113–114, the twenty-third and twenty-fourth, Sun–Mon, the fen road, the March road, the first break, the second break, the yard, ~2,640)
**The March road walked on the Sunday by three of them with a bar, a line, a spade and a bucket.** Eleven miles of fen road, then four bad miles of March road: standing water in the ditches, and a bad half mile where the ditches have gone and the water has come up out of the ground. **The first break, a mile short of the low dry: about forty yards of gone ground, forty yards, the sides slumped, standing water to the depth of a boot, gravel that has had a foot of water stood on it.** **The second break, a quarter mile past the low dry: running sand with a soft rise at the far end, and a man who has got a cart across it with a team and a plank and says he would not do it again.** At the sand, **Jonas Byre says that Ivo Tarrant's four men would have dug the low quarter ditch in a night and did not, and that we refused them on five correct grounds, and asks for it entered that he said it correctly and has thought about it every day.** Monday: **two carts hired at five shillings a day each and two carters' men at two, fourteen shillings a day, wanted on the morning, and the seed house tin counted out on the step in front of two people — fourteen shillings, and a day's carts, and a shilling's copy of the printing is what made it fourteen.** The levy is put at **four times what it was, eighteen pounds, exactly as Jonas Byre predicted it would be on the fifth of a month two months back, and Anneke Roos asks for both halves of that in one paragraph; thirteen pounds four came in and four pounds sixteen did not, and the four pounds sixteen is entered as not received and not remitted and not carried as a debt against anybody.** A carters' chain is mended by **Wil Tam, mender of implements, who keeps a bothy at the crossing: two shillings for a cold rivet and nothing at all for a split tire, and the reason — you have not had a tire off me before and I would like there to be a first one that cost you nothing.** A load is put over the first break on a plank in eleven minutes. **And then the sentence that decides the day, from a man with nineteen years at it: you can go over loaded and you cannot come back loaded, ever, not this month and not in the spring — a loaded cart presses the water down and it comes up on the far side, and a loaded cart coming back would put the wheels in the hole it made on the way out.** Marek decides the loaded carts do not go this month. **Sixteen shillings spent and nothing moved.** At dusk, **Jonas Byre's objection, entered with his name: the fen is paying to move a thing it has not got, over a road it does not own, to a place it has never seen — and Marek Vale says the sentence, not him: a bridge with no return is a road with crates on it, and Jonas Byre says write that down, because I did not say it and you did.** The fifteenth note.

**82. The First Crate** (days 115–116, the twenty-fifth and twenty-sixth, Tue–Wed, the yard, the north ditch, the well house door, ~2,660)
**The declared capacity goes into the schedule in the trade's words, and the works go up: eleven willow sets on the north ditch of the seed house ground with a fiber bed twenty-two yards between them, four new posts and a re-lashed roof on the compost bay, and a board on two stakes at the north gate with a second board under it, empty, for a copy, not nailed to anything because there is no copy at the other end.** The withy came off the south embankment windbreak and somebody said the word and nobody said anything after it. **The declared figure, the twenty-fifth, the ninth hour, read aloud by the man of about fifty: thirty crates a month out and thirty a month back, being a cart a day on the working days of a month, and the return is entered in the seventh column and is not part of this figure.** **Coll Renner comes on the twenty-sixth with his own clip and no case, to see a set of willow and not a person, and the contact is at the eleventh willow set, the instrument against green withy, the purpose in the use-log line entered as *the return*, the contact tested with a plain wire gauge and the test written down first, and the line is the twelfth and records the contact and the hour and the roll and nothing whatever about what came out.** Nine seconds, which he could not account for any better than the eleven at the head or the four on a cut face. **The batch's one panel, in the instrument and not in a person:** *A return is a second keeper, not a second cart. / The first keeper gives. The second keeper sends something back. / If the second end sends nothing, the first end is a road.* **Renner reads them twice and does not read them out loud, and gives the reason: he read three lines out loud in a yard in this room a season ago with a foreman who cannot read standing at the end of the table, and he would rather be the second.** Nothing is written down. Then the measurement, on the district's own discipline, in public, at the well house door: **the load entered before the reading, then seven items read out one at a time by a person, and the reading is six.** *Capacity, measured, the twenty-sixth: six crates out in a month and none back. Six is a fifth of thirty.* **Jonas Byre says that is the reason he has been waiting nine days for, and that he is going to close the book, and cannot, and asks that the reason be entered, and the woman with the shilling gives it in one sentence: because you can only close a book by writing in it, and then you are the man who closed it, and that is a name in a book anybody may read.** The return column is read empty, and the number of the readings is written under the form: ten.

**83. The Dry End** (days 117–118, the twenty-seventh and twenty-eighth, Thu–Fri, the yard, the first break, the low dry, Wil Tam's bothy, Wrenlow, the yard, ~2,730)
**The fen does not go, and the reason is four months old.** Marek: if I stand on that ground I will have seen it, and everything this holding says about it will be a thing a man who has been there said.
