And a thing with no number under it and a road on it is not a nest, and the word you want is the word for a thing that has to be answered to, and you will not use it because using it means somebody is going to have to answer, and nobody in this district has ever had to answer a thing about a floor.** Somebody asks him what the word is and he says no in one word, and then he gives **the half that is checkable**: **a thing under that ground does not go where the ground goes, and it is doing work, and the work is not water and is not seeds and is not anything anybody in this yard can name, and it is the sort of work that stops if nobody does it.** And he will not say what it is, and he has said no in one word to four offices in nineteen years and has never had to say it twice to the same one. **And then the ask, in nine words, with the reason entered as the reason, which is that there is none he is willing to put in a book of a body of sixteen households on one head.** Lissa Vale asks the only question that is hers to ask — **who brings it back** — and he says **not him**, and the clerk is told to enter it in the form he said it and not to let anybody in the book say later that he promised. And then he does the thing nobody expected: **he says the price out loud before anybody asks him to. One pail. And the losing of a man you have had for four days, who has not been paid and is not going to be paid and is not asking, and who has told you two things in a dark yard with a lamp on it that are worth about four years of work to a man who does this. That is the whole of the price and I am telling you in front of four people so that you can decide whether it is one you want to pay, and not one of those four is going to have an opinion and I would like that entered too.** Nobody had an opinion. Then the concealment, at the eleventh hour, in a room with four people of whom one is the clerk: **the engineer of record told the seedwright of the trust that the ground under the low quarter is going to move before the end of next month, and he said the eleventh, and he cannot defend it and has not got a second reading. She said: enter it. He said: I will enter it when I can defend it.** And her sentence, once, and the reader of this body was not in the room and has never read it back: **a thing that is true and in one room is not a secret yet. The day it is in the same room again is the day it is a secret, and I am telling you the day now so that you cannot have it later, and I am not going to be thanked for it and I have said that before I was thanked.** And: **I am not going to write it down, and the reason is that a thing that one person can carry is not a thing a body can be asked to carry, and that is the sixth time in a year I have said a sentence of that shape and the sixth time I have refused to have it written as a refusal.** So there is a date in this book and there is no figure against it. And then the Friday: **a body of sixteen households on one head finds out what it has, which is two pails, counted twice by the examiner on the Friday morning, and one of them belongs to a woman and one of them is carrying three people's work** — and the man of about fifty does the whole of it in about four sentences, the shortest thing he has said in a season: **we have two pails and there is no such pail as this holding's pail, and I am not going to have a yard spend a week on it; we have no pail and never have and I have never once in eleven years counted them and I counted them this morning because a stranger asked for one, and that is the correct order to find things out in and I would like it entered as a fault and not as a lesson; and a pail is not the thing that matters, because he asked for a pail, a measure and an hour, and the pail is the smallest of the three and the hour is the whole of it, and nobody in this yard knows what the hour is for, and I have never once agreed to a thing I could not name.** Panel: none. Notes: none.
- **157 — *The Hour*** — days 266–268, the twenty-sixth, the twenty-seventh and the twenty-eighth. **The batch's completed beat, and it costs.** On the twenty-sixth at about the third hour of the afternoon, **in a yard with about thirty people in it, which is the largest this holding has had on a working day since the flood, this body gives away a pail, a measure and four hours of a licensed man's time, and enters all three, and the hour is fixed for the fourth hour of the twenty-eighth to the eighth hour of it.** The reader puts them as three things and will not have them put as a gift: **one, this holding has two pails and has had for eleven years and nobody knew until Friday morning when a stranger asked for one, and that is a fault and not a lesson; two, the boards pail, washed, and the wash is entered because the last thing it carried was a man's fever and the man does not want it recorded; three, the pail may not come back, he said so on Thursday in front of four people, the steward asked who would bring it back and he said not him, and nobody in this yard has been given a promise and has been given a time.** Then the steward's second answer this holding has ever given that it could not finish, and it is entered with its reason: **on the seventh of last month I could not tell you what four carts cost anybody, and today I cannot tell you what a held ground costs, what nine people and four days of water cost, what twenty-six trays cost a woman who carried them eleven miles, what seventeen pounds, eight shillings and fourpence of road costs four hundred and forty-one people, or what a man with a cart will do on the twenty-eighth with a pail and an hour. And what has changed is that this holding is now a party to one thing it cannot price, and that is the first time in eleven years I have had to stand in a yard and admit that a thing I have done has a price I have not got.** And a licensed man refuses to go, and the reason is that **a licensed man under a ground is a thing that can be written about afterwards, and I would rather there were four people in this yard who can say afterwards that there was no licensed man under that ground at any hour on the twenty-eighth** — and he will be at both hours and will not write down what happened in between and has not asked. On the twenty-seventh, in a room of nine, **the reader asks the question of the batch: is there anything in this holding's book that you know and that is not in this holding's book — and the engineer of record says yes, and is told not to be asked what it is, and gives the count and the day and not the thing: four answers in a season, and the count of the four that are in this book's book is one, and the fourth one is dated the eleventh of next month.** And the reader says the other half of it first, and it is the volume's fault line: **a man who cannot defend a thing and holds it anyway has not kept a private record, he has started one — and a body that has a name can hold a thing it cannot defend, and that is how a name becomes a party, and I said in a yard on the eleventh of this month that a name is a thing other people can spend, and I did not think about that for nine days and I have thought about it for one, and I would like somebody in four years to find the two pages and read them side by side.** Nobody answered him and nobody was asked. On the twenty-eighth the standing figures are read with nothing softened — **no compost line, the leak at three-quarters with no verdict on the difference, the beat not logged since, the seventh column at thirty-one, the boards at two hundred and two, the tin at two hundred and forty-nine, the rule four lines used once and used outside one of them, the contact and its cost and the rootmark unchanged, the well on the shelf at five feet and one inch and three feet and eleven inches not decomposed, the day letter and the green and the fourth line with nothing entered four times, and a pail, a measure and four hours gone.** **The man of about seventy is fetched for the twelfth time in a season and says three words: write it twice**, and nobody explained it and the fen does not know what it means. And then the volume's third question, in the fen's own words, **and the page is careful that it is not the first two: the first question asks who gave an order, and the third does not ask that, and nobody in this book could answer it, because an order has a person at the end of it and this body has not got one and has looked and not found one.** The count of the times it has been asked is one and the count of the times it has been available to a room and not taken is one, and **the two counts are not to be added to the count of the first question, which stands at two asked and three available and not taken, and the count of a question is the count of a question.** The **forty-fourth** note closes the month and carries the standing sentence. Panel: none. Notes: the **forty-fourth**, dated **the twenty-eighth**, **carrying the standing sentence.**

**The batch is 28,503 words by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings, range 2,599–3,193, inside the outline's 27,000–30,000. The per-chapter standard is the outline's 2,600–3,120 and two chapters sit outside it and are declared here rather than absorbed: Ch 151 at 2,599 is one word under the floor and is the shortest, filled with the wall, the four reasons and the refusals and not with summary, and Ch 157 at 3,193 is 73 over the ceiling. **The band was NOT widened to cover them.** The previous version of this line reported “every chapter inside 2,550–3,200”, which was a re-baseline and not a measurement, and it has been withdrawn; Volume 03 closed on 2,550–3,120 and that is the floor and ceiling a later writer is held to, with 2,600–3,120 as the target. Ch 157 at 3,193 and Ch 155 at 3,107 may not be lengthened, and **Ch 157 was left long on purpose rather than trimmed seventy-three words**, because every candidate in it is either a speech of the chapter or the standing figures and the section-nine note, which are this manuscript's own ritual, and a trim there would have cost a fact to buy a number. Ch 157 was drafted at 3,983 and came out of the drafting pass at 3,193 after nine trims of restatement, and nothing that carries a fact, a name, a date or a beat was cut.** Mechanical check: **no paragraph ends with a speech open in any of the ten files**, thirteen single-paragraph speeches having been drafted with an opening mark and no close and all thirteen corrected by appending the missing mark, **and the even-odd count of marks is not a test of anything in this manuscript and was not run**; **no non-ASCII glyph at all**, so no curly mark and no em dash; no month named in narration or in any document, **the rule being the outline's** — a day is an ordinal, *of this month* or *of last month*, a named month is not carried in a distance, and a day of the first month is a bare number only, while the ordinal distances (*a month two months back* and the like) are the house idiom and are untouched — and the only capitalised *March* being *the March's tin*, *the March road*, *the Salt March Dryfoot Trust, of Cray* and once *nine thousand people in the March*; the word *seat* does not appear; no Crown Engine, no Iona Vey, no False Season, no Pale Orchard, no Quiet Break, no Continuity Office, no Halden Pike, no Tarin Callow, no Assemtry; **the name *Brinewake* is nowhere in the batch and Thornwild is nowhere in the batch**; no British spellings. **Two duplicate sentences of nine words or more across all ten files, and both are deliberate, so the earlier claim of zero was false and is withdrawn:** the guarantee's second clause, verbatim in the document and then said aloud in speech (Ch 149), which is a clause being read out of a sheet in a room and is the scene, and the standing figures refrain *day minus nineteen unmoved on a bed at three and a half degrees both undertakings standing* (Ch 154 and Ch 157). **The property worth auditing is that no paragraph ends with a speech open, and that holds.** **The arithmetic was checked line by line and two errors were found and repaired on the page, both of them pre-decimal coin: one shilling is twelve pence, so one shilling and fourpence is sixteen pence and the margin over eleven days is seventeen pounds, eight shillings and fourpence and not twenty pounds and eighteen shillings, and the tariff line in the return is seven pence and nine pence and not nineteen pence; and the projection of the bill to the thirtieth is two thousand eight hundred and fifty pails and one hundred and forty-two pounds ten and not one hundred and forty-one pounds six.** **Twenty locks respected and every one of them is checkable on the page.** Volume 03 stands at 142,952 words and 450,792 across 147 chapters, both untouched, and Volume 04 Batch 0001 adds 28,503, so the manuscript stands at **479,295 words across 157 chapters**.

# VOLUME 04 — BATCH 0002, CHAPTERS 158–167, MOVEMENT 2: WHAT SHE SAID IN A ROOM

**Ten chapters, days 269–285, the twenty-ninth and the thirtieth of the ninth month through the fifteenth of the tenth, and the batch crosses the month turn between Ch 158 and Ch 159 and therefore has TWO naming tables and not one. The volume's outline is `outline/volume-04.md` and the governing prompt for this batch is `workspace/volume-04/batch-0002/PROMPT.md`, which was written by the phase that drafted Batch 0001 and carries the ten cards. The next prompt is `workspace/volume-04/batch-0003/PROMPT.md`.**

- **158 — *The Thirtieth*** — days 269–270, the twenty-ninth and the thirtieth, a Tuesday and a Wednesday. **The batch's first fact is a fact about a floor, told flat and at the front: the hour on the twenty-eighth has run, a licensed man was at the fourth hour and at the eighth and wrote both down, a measure and a plain wire gauge were on the boards at the eighth, the pail was not, and nobody came with either.** Coll Renner gives the hour before the finding and gives the finding before anything else, and puts *nobody came with either* in the same line as the two of them because a person is not what came or did not come. **The reader of this body then splits the sentence in two and has the two halves entered apart: nobody in that yard can say what the hour was for, and that is a fact; the fen has decided this morning not to ask him and not to write to anybody about it, and that is a decision.** The man of about thirty-five has said he will not be asked again by this body and would say no in one word to a person in an office. Then the last reading day of the ninth month: **the door read out whole at the tenth hour in a full yard of about twenty-five, by the steward, with the reader and the clerk, the count of the readings of the seventh column going to thirty-two**, the compost line for the month not entered and the reason entered (**the figure falls on the first of next month, a Thursday, and this holding has said so out loud on the eleventh, the sixteenth, the twenty-first, the twenty-ninth and the thirtieth of the ninth month and the count of the five is entered**), the leak at three-quarters of an inch with no verdict, the beat not logged since with the column open, **the boards at two hundred and four days, day minus sixty-six, and the March's tin at two hundred and fifty-one, day minus nineteen, both cut or read out with the day-minus rule read out loud first, which the reader has now done on four reading days running and which nobody asked for**, and the standing rule read out in full again, four lines, before the door is closed. And then the question the chapter is for, asked standing and not taken: **was the hour.** Nobody answers it, nobody is asked to, and **the count of the questions available to a room in this holding and not taken goes to five**, the first in a month two months back and the fourth in a low ground on the twenty-third, and the reader says he is not going to ask it twice and enters the reason, that he has no standing to ask after it. And the steward's **third answer this holding has ever given that it could not finish**, and the difference between it and the first two is on the page: **the first was what four carts cost, the second was what four hours cost, and this one is what a thing this body cannot ask anybody about cost, and a body that has given away a thing and got half of it back is not in the same position as a body that was refused, and there is no sentence in this book's vocabulary for that and I am not going to invent one in a yard.** Panel: none. Notes: none.
- **159 — *The First Of The Tenth*** — day 271, the first of the tenth month, a Thursday, which is also the day the ninth month's compost line falls on, and the page says both are on the same day and is not going to make anything of it. The line is entered at about the eleventh hour in a yard of about twenty, by the examiner, in the steward's book, in her own hand, which is not the steward's hand, with the reader appointed being the man of about fifty: **specification four hundred hundredweight; the going, nothing, and the reason entered, that this holding hired nobody in the ninth month and paid off nobody hired in it, and the examiner states she has been in the hiring book twice and did not believe herself the first time; shortfall four hundred hundredweight, eighty pounds, at four shillings the hundredweight, which is the compost valuation and is not anybody's day rate; the run of it is nine months and it has not been discharged in nine.** And the batch's arithmetic beat, said before the figure and without any pleasure in it: **it is emptier, not better — the first month in nine that has not gone up, and it has not gone up because there was nothing in it to charge.** The two sums, not averaged and not in one column: **four hundred at four shillings is one thousand six hundred shillings, eighty pounds; and four hundred at the price at Cray, three shillings and elevenpence, being forty-seven pence, is eighteen thousand eight hundred pence, which is one thousand five hundred and sixty-six shillings and eightpence, seventy-eight pounds six shillings and eightpence; the difference is one pound, thirteen shillings and fourpence, and the whole of that difference is a penny that came down by itself on a sheet with a firm's name at the head of it on the first of last month, and this holding did not cause it and did not write and ask — and a body that puts that penny on the same line as a hundredweight specification is a book that has stopped being a book.** The reader reads the run aloud, ninety pounds eight, eighty pounds eight, ninety-five, eighty, and enters that a fall of fifteen pounds from the month before is not good news and is the same line. Then the batch's whole clock, four sentences at the end: **the reader of this body says the date out loud in a yard of about twenty, in the ordinary voice, and says three days, and the examiner says ten days and not three and says before anything is written down that she keeps time and does not round, and he agrees with her, and both figures are entered and neither is improved on.** He does not say whose date it is and does not say what it is about, and does not ask the engineer of record. **The man of about seventy is at the wall and is not fetched, and is given the two sums, the two figures and the difference, and says nothing, and the fen entered that it did not ask him whether he had anything to say, which is his standing and has never once cost anybody anything.** Panel: none. Notes: none.
- **160 — *Two And A Bit Times*** — days 272–273, the second and the third, a Friday and a Saturday. **A schedule comes up the fen road on the ordinary post from the holder of record of a well eleven miles off, in a clerk's hand, with the days listed and a figure at the foot, and it is correct, and the holder's man is not on it and the holder is.** Four lines: a supply is made on a day on which the wells of the quarter served are off and is not made on a day on which they are on; **drawn, the first to the eleventh of last month, eleven days, and the nineteen to the twenty-third, five days; sixteen days at ninety-five the hundredweight a day: one thousand five hundred and twenty hundredweight**; and the ordinary-course line with the form outstanding. **The reader finds the seven missing days in the middle and the seven in the tail, and the first line explains them, and then says the thing this holding cannot do about them: it cannot count fourteen days of wells in a quarter of four hundred and forty-one people, and the fourteen days at the top of that sheet are the most checkable-looking part of it and the part this body cannot check at all, and I have read a schedule and I have not read fourteen days of that quarter's ground.** Then the batch's best piece of arithmetic, done twice out loud and both derivations entered: **at one shilling and fourpence, being sixteen pence, one thousand five hundred and twenty hundredweight is twenty-four thousand three hundred and twenty pence, which is two thousand and twenty-six shillings and eightpence, which is one hundred and one pounds, six shillings and eightpence; they paid one thousand five hundred and twenty shillings, which is seventy-six pounds; the difference is twenty-five pounds, six shillings and eightpence. And fourpence a hundredweight on one thousand five hundred and twenty is six thousand and eighty pence, which is five hundred and six shillings and eightpence, which is twenty-five pounds six shillings and eightpence. Same.** And that it is a road, eleven miles, in nobody's pocket, with four hundred and forty-one people at the end of it. And then the half that is the chapter: **this body has carried a pail as a hundredweight for two months as a practice and not as a fact, and a clerk in another building has carried it as a hundredweight as a fact, and the two now agree, and agreeing is not the same as either of them being right** — and the count of one thousand and forty-five counted at a gate by four households over the first eleven days and one thousand and forty-five hundredweight on that same sheet for those same eleven days is **a coincidence and not a verification**. And the reader's sentence: **a body that cannot do the sum is a body that has a complaint; a body that can do the sum and cannot write the name is a body that has a figure, and a figure is a thing an office can use against a body in a room.** **The count of questions asked in a yard and not got an answer goes to three.** Panel: none. Notes: none.
- **161 — *She Came On The Fourth*** — days 274–275, the fourth and the fifth, a Sunday and a Monday. **A retired root engineer of fifty-six comes up the fen road in the rain on foot and is not announced, and the reason she was not announced is that she is already in this book's book as the first of the three names on the certificate in that room, and a body that announces at its own gate a person already on a page in its own book has admitted it does not know its own book.** She came by the ordinary post and walked the last nine miles, and the fen entered that as a fact and did not ask her about it. **She gives her name and her position in the first minute in a form that could be read back, and she says the fee before anybody offers it: nothing, and I am not charging you, and I am saying that first and not later, and a fee is the only thing a person in my position can charge and I have not charged it.** And the reason, which is the professional reason for the thing the reader of this body said in a yard eight days earlier: **a body that has been told its record cannot be used is a body that will start keeping another kind, and the other kind does not have a reading day and does not have an hour on it and is kept in a man's own book, and I have watched two bodies do that in nineteen years and one of them was a good body and the other one is the reason I am standing in yours.** Nobody thanked her and she said before she was thanked that she was not going to be; the count of that standing in a season is twelve. **The fen entered that it has not paid her, cannot pay her, and would not have been able to, and that this is the reason she came, in her own words and not improved.** The reader asks the one question nobody else in that yard can ask, giving the reason first: **are you here because of a declaration or because of a name.** Neither, and the third thing, and then she says she is not going to be forgiven and has not come to be thanked and has not come to be given a room, a bed or a figure. And then at the ninth hour the steward refuses her the seed house **in one sentence**: *a person who is not of this body does not sleep in the seed house* — with the reason entered, and **the reason is not suspicion, and is that an exception made this week is an exception made, and the next one is easier, and a seed house is not a place to put a custom in, and the same sentence would have been given to a licensed man off an office who had come up the fen road in the rain with a form in his bag.** She accepted it in about four seconds, which the fen counted, and **said nothing at all about it for the rest of the evening and was not thanked and did not ask to be.** On the Monday she stands at the wall and asks for the page, and the steward gives it to her without asking the reader or the clerk, and the reader enters that afterwards and has not written and asked why. And the reason she came, in her own words: **four sources, four hands, and not one of them is a clerk, and I have watched this holding be proud of that for eleven years and it is the single most useful thing I have ever seen a body do.** Panel: none. Notes: none.
- **162 — *The Name In Her Own Mouth*** — day 276, the sixth, a Tuesday. **A room with the bay doors shut and about eleven people in it, and a woman of fifty-six at the side of the table where the light is worst, and the man of about seventy is not in the room and the fen entered that it did not fetch him for the thirteenth time in a season because it has brought him out once in nine days and once in two days and is not going to do it a third time to find out what he would say about a word he has never heard.** She gives the four decisions in order and the number and then one word: **nineteen years ago she designed a safety relay for a flow in a dry country; the relay could have isolated a pulse going out where it should not have gone; she took the red line out of it; she changed the log entry from what it was to a line that said routine recalibration; she signed a report that put the cause down to salt creep and weather. Four thousand two hundred people in the first winter and nine thousand more moved. And then she said the name of the place, once, out of her own mouth, and did not say it again, and the clerk wrote it once and did not write it twice.** Then the reader asks what she wants this body to do with a number like that, and she says **nothing**, and wants that written down first, because everything else in that room has been about what people are going to do. And then the discipline: **we cannot check one word of it; there are two things in the world that can carry what she has said, one of them is her memory and the other is a record of compensation that is sealed and is produced to the person named in it on that person's own request, and no request has been made, nobody has been named, nothing has been opened, and it is not going to be opened in this room.** Tova Reed's standing, once, unnumbered: **a mechanism is not a verification and a soundness is not a hand, and this holding is about to enter the soundness of a woman into its own book and call the book a record.** And the cost. **She asks for the one thing she came for and it is not money: to be entered in this holding's own book, under her own name, with the date, as the person who said it — and the reason she gives is that in four years there is going to be one person who can say she said it, and if she is not on the page then the page says a thing happened to a room.** And the reader puts both halves in front of about eleven people and names the wrong half as wrong before he says it: **if this holding writes it down, an office can list it, and a thing in a list is a thing that has been had; and if it does not, then a woman said a thing in a room and it is gone, and in four years she will be the only person who ever said it and this book will be the proof that nobody was listening, and that is worse than the list.** **Enter it.** And the whole of the cost, in his own words, with his hand on the bench: **this body has been a name for a month and has just made itself a party, and I said the sentence about how that happens eight days before a woman walked nine miles to hand it to us, and I did not think about it for a day then and I have thought about it for two days since and I am not going to improve on it in a room with eleven people in it.** Nobody thanked anybody. Panel: none. Notes: none.
- **163 — *A Party That Named Itself*** — days 277–278, the seventh and the eighth, a Wednesday and a Thursday. **A return comes on the ordinary post, four lines, correct, and the third line has four words in it that are the name of a party, and a document is the only place a body can be a party.** Water supplied into the low quarter at no price between the second and the seventh, out of a source the return does not name, no price charged, the party named, the ordinary-course line: **a party supplying at no price is a party and is entered as one, and a party is not a holder, and a party has the standing of a party and not of a holder and not of an office. The name is the Pale Orchard.** Nobody in that room said the word *gift* and the fen entered that nobody said it and entered the word twice in its own book for the sole purpose of not using it. And the arithmetic, which is the second sum and the chapter's real argument: **the low quarter had five wells off on the fourth day of this month, and from the second to the seventh it has had the licensed supply at a shilling a pail and for every one of those six days water at no price out of a source nobody has named, and the number of pails in the second supply is in no book because there was no gate — I can add the two and I cannot divide them, and a figure that is the sum of two things one of which was never measured is not a figure, it is a shape, and I am not going to enter it and I am not going to be asked to.** And the three not-askings put side by side on one page on purpose: **this body's, over the penny; the trust's, over a figure carried by one person; the haulier's, over a holder's man with a book** — and a person in four years is going to read the three together and tell what sort of body this is. And then six sentences from a household's own mouth, from the low quarter: **the same four men were standing in that yard on the seventh and the sentence was gone, and one of the four said why, and the four words are: they gave us water, a person does not say a thing like that with water in the house; nobody at that gate asked her whether she still thought it, and he did not ask her, and the fen is glad, and a body being glad is not a column and there is no form for it.** And the man of about seventy fetched for the thirteenth time in a season, given the four lines and the two halves of the sum, and asked the only question in the room — **is water at no price better than water at a price** — which nobody can answer; he then said four words, **ask the giver's name**, and the fen does not know what they were about and has entered that it does not know. And the reader's position: **a body that cannot name a party on a form is now in a worse position than it was a week ago, because there is a party on a form that this holding cannot name either, and the two are going to be read in the same room one day by somebody who has not been born yet — and I am not going to say the second half and I have decided not to say it and that is a decision and not an oversight.** Panel: none. Notes: none.
- **164 — *A Holder In A Yard*** — day 279, the ninth, a Friday. **Garrow Nye, holder of record of the Pans well, of no address this office holds, comes up the fen road in the rain with a schedule in a leather bag and a man with him, and is let in at the gate and announced, because he has not been in this yard in his life and this holding has no custom for a charter holder and had to invent one at the gate in about four seconds.** About twenty-five people in his yard inside ten minutes. **He is not a liar and he corrects this holding twice in his first ten minutes, both against himself, and the reader enters the count and enters that the two are not to be improved on by anybody who was not in that yard:** the licensed draw on that well in the last twelve months is **one hundred and ninety thousand hundredweight** against a charter specification of **one hundred and seventy-four thousand**, and he has put the two in a room together and looked at them, and it is the first time in nineteen years; and **the man with the cart is licensed to carry under his charter, which he did not know until the morning he came up the road, and which is in the schedule in his bag and which he is not going to give out, because a schedule is a holder's document.** Then what he came to say, in five sentences: the well stopped at the fourth hour of the morning of the twentieth of last month and the book is at home and is anybody's book to read; **the flow under the low quarter and the flow under the Pans well are the same flow, and I do not know how a body of sixteen households finds that out and I have not asked how**; the man with the cart is not the cause of anything, I am; **and a form would have said it wrong and a form would have said it late, and I have had four days to write it down and have not written it down, and if you want to make a thing of it you are going to have to make a thing of it the hard way.** And then the reader puts the volume's frame back into a yard of twenty-five before the ask can take it out: **a charter holder is a supply and is not a thief, and nobody in that yard said the word *thief* about anybody during the ten minutes that man was speaking, and the fen did not find that out by asking.** Then the ask: **I want this holding to certify, on a form, that a flow is broken**, and the form is the ordinary form, correct, four lines, filled in with a date, a well, a licensed man and a number; **and he knows the form would close the licensed draw, which is the only supply that quarter has got, and that the water a party put in there at no price has stopped and nobody knows where it came from or where it went, and he has brought it in the rain anyway — a holder whose well is broken and who does not say so is a holder whose break is somebody else's water, and I have read four holders' books and can tell which of them were lying and I do not think I am one of them, and I would like that thought about with a form on it in front of twenty-five people.** And the batch's one Fieldbook panel, spent here, at the fourth hour, at the shaft in this holding's own low ground: **the use log's fifteenth line written first and read back first by the person who keeps the seed house**, a plain wire gauge test written down before the instrument was laid on the boards, four lines and not three — *The mouth is not the same twice. / A flow that is broken shows the break and not the hand that made it. / A person who says it out loud is not a finding. / Ask what it is carrying and not who is holding it* — **and the licensed man says out loud before he reads them that the second line is about a hand and not about a person and that no document in this book is going to quote them as an instruction or as a reason**, and the cost: the mark hot, the hand from the wrist down no use for about a day, the case carried up the slope by the man from the north row. And what he says up in the yard with twenty-five people in it: **this contact says nothing whatsoever about the Pans well, not one word of it, and the only man in this district who could tell me what is carrying under that floor is a man of about thirty-five who has said he will not be asked again by this body.** And the decision, at the seventh hour, entered and read back twice: **this holding will not put its name to the form and will not refuse to either; the form is on the bench; it has not been returned and it has not been torn up; the entry is that the form is on the bench and the answer has not been given; and the day the answer is given, a holder of record will have an answer from a body of sixteen households with no charter.** Panel: the one available, spent here. Notes: none.
- **165 — *The Eleventh*** — days 280–281, the tenth and the eleventh, a Saturday and a Sunday. **The tenth is a Saturday and nothing is read out in it. The eleventh is a Sunday and on the Sunday nothing happened that this body can see, and the fen entered that on the eleventh that it cannot say what happened and did not say the prediction was wrong and entered that it did not say it.** The standing figures of the day are read in the ordinary way with about nine people, none of them softened, **and the date is not on the list, and the clerk read a line under the list out of a standing instruction, and nobody in that yard asked what the line meant because about nine people in a fen of sixteen households knew what it meant.** The reader's reason, to nine people, with the second half before anybody can get to the first: **a prediction that comes and goes with nothing attached to it is not proved wrong by having nothing happen on the day; it is only proved wrong on a day when something does happen and it was not written down. A concealment was worth something on the twenty-fourth of last month, when the man doing it could not defend it and said so out loud to about four people in a room, and it is worth nothing today, and the man who did it is the one who has to say that, and he is in this yard, and I would like the clerk to enter that I said that in front of him and not behind his back.** Then the engineer of record, four sentences: **I said it to one person in a room and I would not write it, and the reason I gave at the time is that I could not defend it and I had not got a second reading, and that reason was a good reason on the twenty-fourth of last month and it is not a good reason today; I am not going to be thanked and I am not going to be forgiven today either, because it is not that kind of day; and I would like the clerk to enter a word nowhere in this book, in this entry or in any other, because nobody in this room is going to promise me anything.** The reader goes in and does not come out, and the seedwright of the trust says the chapter's sentence to nine people in the yard without him: **a thing that is true and in one room is not a secret yet, the day it is in the same room again is the day it is a secret — and here is the half I have not said in that room, and a thing that has been in one room for a month and has come true and has not been written down is not a secret, it is a debt; and I am not going to be the person you tell the next one to, and I am not going to be angry about it in front of nine people, and if anybody in this room wants to be angry about it in front of nine people I will help them and it will take about four minutes.** **She is not thanked and she did not ask to be, and the count of a sentence of that shape in a year is six, and the reader of this body was not in the room when she said it.** Then at about the fourth hour of the afternoon the man of about thirty-five comes down into the low ground on foot with nothing that was asked for: **I have been on that ground for nine days and there is more of it than there was on the twenty-second, and I am telling you that because you gave me a pail and I have not been paid and I said I would not be asked again — nobody asked me, I came down because I have got one count and I do not want it to be a thing I was asked for.** Nobody thanked him, **and the fen entered that the pail has not come back and the four hours have not been paid for and that the two of those are on the same page, and that the payment has arrived as information and this holding does not know what to do with a payment that arrives as information and is not going to put it in a column.** And one sentence that is not the word he has refused and is not an answer to anything — **it is not late** — and nobody in that low ground knew what that meant and the fen entered that it does not know and has not written and asked. Panel: none. Notes: none.
- **166 — *Not A Fifth Line*** — days 282–283, the twelfth and the thirteenth, a Monday and a Tuesday. **The standing list of four lines is read out whole and read back whole in the seed house with the bay doors shut and about eleven people in the room, in the same words it was last read out in, on the ninth of last month, when a fourth line was added to a list that had had three lines in it for eleven years.** Nobody offered to change a word of it and the count of that is one. Then the reader asks the third line out loud on purpose, before he can say anything about the fourth one, and the count goes on the page: **what this body does not know is four, it was four a month ago, and it is not going to be three this month** — a flow under a floor four hundred yards from the gate, a fourth cart that went up a road in the eighth month and has not come down and that nobody asked about, fourteen days of a quarter that nobody counted, and a thing a man of about thirty-five has refused to say for nineteen years, **and the two lines are different and I am not going to let the two run together this month, and that is the reason the fourth line is not the third line.** Then the question the list has never been asked, with the reason first, and then he answers it himself so that nobody in the room has to be first: **five things. One, a shelf four hundred miles off, four houses, a yard, a wall with eight figures on it, a row of eleven faces and a row nobody has read, and a woman of fifty-four with a nail. Two, a yard four hundred miles off, the same wall, the same nail, nine of the eleven faces read with a rule and two not read and a stake in the ground at each. Three, a form on a bench in this room with a date and a well and a licensed man and a number on it, and a man of about eleven miles' road who has brought it in the rain and has not been answered. Four, a woman in this room who has not been forgiven and has not asked. Five, a name at the foot of a sheet in eleven miles that this body has no address for, and that this body has read out loud in a yard once this month and did not ask the man whether he wanted it read out loud.** And then the refusal, short, and the fen enters it: **a line is a standing, a standing is a thing this holding has to keep, this holding has no charter and four pounds and fivepence and a pail it has lost, and a body that adds a line to a standing list because a thing arrived is a body that has stopped keeping a list and started keeping a diary.** **Four lines and five things and the count of the difference is one, entered as a difference and not as a decision, because this body has not decided which of the five it is going to stop looking after and is not going to decide it in a room with about eleven people in it.** And the batch's second refusal, Tova Reed's, asked in the ordinary way and without preamble — **to be the person the engineer of record comes to when he has a figure he cannot defend** — refused in one sentence: **I am the person you bring a figure to when it is a figure. I am not the person you bring a thing to when it is not one, and you have known the difference between those two since the fourteenth of last month and so have I, and if you cannot hold the difference then you do not need me, you need a book, and you have one, and it is on the middle of this table and anybody may read it.** **The reader of this body was not in that room. The engineer of record did not answer her. Nobody in that room answered her. The time between the question and the end of the room is about nine seconds and the fen entered it and does not know what to do with it.** And then, at the seventh hour, in a yard of about nine, the other half, said out loud because a thing has to go into a yard before it is a thing everybody knows: **this holding has spent a month learning that a body with no standing can still be asked to put its name to a form, and a month learning that a man with a private record is a party, and it has not yet learned the third thing, which is that a person who is offered the job of being the one you tell is going to be offered it again, and is going to say no again, and the second no is easier and the third one is not.** Panel: none. Notes: none.
- **167 — *Entered, In Her Own Name*** — days 284–285, the fourteenth and the fifteenth, a Wednesday and a Thursday. **The movement closes with the entry read out whole in the seed house, in a room of about eleven, in the words it is written in: four lines, the longest single entry in this holding's history, read back twice, and the reader asked no question about it and the fen entered that he asked none — a thing in this book that a person said out loud in a room is not evidence and is not going to become evidence by being asked about in a yard, and the asking is for the asker and not for the page. There is a blank in the second line of that entry and it has been in the book since the sixth, and the clerk put it there and has not filled it in since, and about eleven people read four lines with a blank in the middle of one of them and nobody asked about the blank.** And the cost, entered as a cost and not a sacrifice: **this holding has no charter, a standing list of four lines, five things it is looking after and cannot reach, four pounds and fivepence, a pail it has lost, a form on a bench with the answer not given, a party named on a return it cannot check, and a woman's name against a thing nobody in this district can corroborate. The payment is this holding's own exposure and it is not hers and it went in this holding's book and not in hers.** And: **she is on this page once, she did not ask to be on it, she was not asked whether she wanted it and would have accepted it either way, and the clerk entered that and read it back and did not improve on it.** Then **the one observation the batch has available and the fifth in the chain: *the book cannot check her*** — entered on the clerk's own motion, unasked, and never explained. Then the figures of the month on the fifteenth, none of them softened: **no compost line for the tenth month and the reason entered; the leak at three-quarters of an inch with no verdict and no measurement since the twenty-first of last month; the beat not logged since with the column open; the seventh column at thirty-two and the last reading day of the month the thirtieth; the boards at two hundred and nineteen, day minus sixty-six; the March's tin at two hundred and sixty-six, day minus nineteen, unmoved, both undertakings standing, the drawer not opened and not to be; the standing rule four lines, used once on the twenty-second of last month and used outside one of its own four lines and no use in this month at all; the use log at fifteen lines; the rootmark four inches and branching twice and unchanged; a form on a bench unanswered; the pail gone and the measure back; and the well on the shelf at five feet and one inch with no entry anywhere for it coming back, and a row, a wall and a yard four hundred miles off with no entry anywhere for any of the three.** And the rule read out in full again in a yard of about nine, **entered as read out three times in twenty-four days and used once, and the count of the uses is one.** And the reader's standing question, asked in the yard, and the fen's answer in six words: **I want somebody to say no** — **and the clerk did not read it back a second time, and the fen entered why, and the reason is that a thing read back twice is a thing that is going to be improved on, and that six words is not a standing and cannot be made into one, and that this holding has six things it has been told no by this season and has not been told no by anybody since the eighteenth of last month and has entered that it does not know which of those two facts it would rather be in.** Then the handoff at the north gate, in four sentences: **the fourth hour on the sixteenth of this month, four hundred miles off, a nail and a string and a woman of fifty-four's own hand; this holding has no standing on it, did not write and ask and is not going to; the reading is not in this book and is not going to be predicted in this book; and the fen entered the day and not the figure, and the day is the sixteenth, and the count of days between the end of this book and the fourth hour of that morning is one, and the fen entered that instead, because a day that can be counted is the only one of the three things this body is able to put in a book.** The **forty-fifth** section-nine note, dated the fifteenth, carrying the standing sentence. Panel: none. Notes: the **forty-fifth**, dated **the fifteenth**, **carrying the standing sentence.**

**The batch is 29,252 words by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings, range 2,692–3,117, and every one of the ten is inside the outline's 2,600–3,120 per-chapter target and inside the outline's 27,000–30,000 for the batch. The band was not widened and no chapter is declared outside it.** The ten figures are **3,098 + 2,882 + 2,844 + 2,879 + 2,976 + 2,692 + 3,117 + 2,727 + 2,956 + 3,081.** Mechanical check: **no paragraph ends with a speech open in any of the ten files.** Four single-paragraph speeches in Chs 159, 163, 165 and 166 were drafted with an opening mark and a bold close and no closing mark, the drafting habit Volume 03's Batch 0005 pass found and corrected, and all four were corrected by appending the missing mark. **The even-odd count of marks in a file is not a test of anything in this manuscript and must not be made even; the property to audit is that no paragraph ends with a speech open, and that holds.** **No non-ASCII glyph at all** in any of the ten files, so no curly mark and no em dash. **No month is named** in narration or in any document, the rule being the outline's — a day is an ordinal, *of this month* or *of last month*, a day of the first month is a bare number only, and the ordinal distances (*a month two months back*, *the sixteenth of last month*, *of this month*) are the house idiom and are untouched — and the only capitalised *March* in the batch is *the March's tin*, twice. The word *seat* does not appear. The word *nest* does not appear. Thornwild is not named. No Crown Engine, no Iona Vey, no False Season, no Continuity Office, no Quiet Break, no Glassward, no Halden Pike, no Tarin Callow, no seedheart, no anchor. **The name *Brinewake* is spoken once in the whole batch, in Chapter 162, out of Sera Quill's own mouth, in a room with about eleven people in it, and is nowhere else in the batch or the volume** — and because the lock forbids the name appearing again after the chapter that spends it, **the four-line entry that is read out whole in Chapter 167 carries a blank where the place-name stands, and the blank is in the book and the clerk has not filled it in since, and the fen entered why and entered that nobody in the room asked about the blank.** No romance and nothing tender on the page. **The arithmetic was checked line by line and all of it holds:** the ninth month's shortfall, four hundred hundredweight at four shillings, is eighty pounds, and the same four hundred at Cray's three shillings and elevenpence is seventy-eight pounds six shillings and eightpence, a difference of one pound thirteen shillings and fourpence; the licensed sum, one thousand five hundred and twenty hundredweight at one shilling and fourpence being sixteen pence, is one hundred and one pounds six shillings and eightpence, against seventy-six pounds paid, a difference of twenty-five pounds six shillings and eightpence, derived a second way as fourpence a hundredweight and agreeing. **One figure in the batch's own card was wrong against the calendar and the page is right:** the card said the prediction is *three days* off when the reader says it on the first of the tenth month, and from the first to the eleventh of a month is ten days; the page has the reader say three, the examiner correct it to ten in front of about twenty people before anything is written down, both figures entered and neither improved on, which is this manuscript's own treatment of a wrong derivation. **Twenty locks respected and every one of them is checkable on the page.** Volume 03 stands at 142,952 words and Volume 04 Batch 0001 at 28,503, both untouched, and Volume 04 Batch 0002 adds 29,252, so the manuscript stands at **508,547 words across 167 chapters**.

## The Volume 04 Batch 0002 review pass — the findings that were applied, and the audit disclosure the pass restored

**The batch is intact. No chapter was restarted and no chapter was replaced. The pass touched nine of the ten files, every change is a correction of a date, a figure, a counter, a word or a name, and every change is recorded here because a corrected fact that is not entered is a fact this book does not have.**

**Five blocking findings, all fixed. (One) Ch 162 forward-dated the reader's own sentence by five days.** He said *On the eleventh of this month I said in a yard that a name is a thing other people can spend*, in a room on the sixth of the tenth month, and Ch 167 dates the same speech to the eleventh of **last** month. **The page now reads the eleventh of last month, and the speech is the same speech, and both of its dates are checkable on an earlier page: Ch 157 has the reader saying in a yard *on the eleventh of this month* that a name is a thing other people can spend, and Ch 157 is in the ninth month, so from the sixth of the tenth the eleventh of last month is the eleventh of the ninth and the other date, the twenty-seventh, is the twenty-seventh of the ninth month and is the day Ch 156 and Ch 157 stand on.** (Two) **Ch 160's central coincidence was contradicted four lines later.** The chapter put one thousand five hundred and twenty beside one thousand five hundred and twenty and then gave the households' own count as one thousand and forty-five, which is the sheet's first window of eleven days at ninety-five, so the figure was not independent and the beat rested on a number the same speech withdrew. **The page now counts the same eleven days twice: four households at a gate got one thousand and forty-five, and a clerk in another building's ninety-five the hundredweight a day for those same eleven days is also one thousand and forty-five, ninety-five times eleven being one thousand and forty-five. The two are the same figure, the thesis sentence stands, and nothing was widened to cover it.** (Three) **Ch 166 said in an official entry that this body did not know four things today and did not know three yesterday, against the speech nine lines above it which says it is not going to be three this month.** The entry now reads **did not know four a month ago**, which is what the speech says and what the standing requires. (Four) **The man of about thirty-five became a woman in Ch 165 and again in Ch 166, and no new woman was introduced anywhere in the batch.** He is a man at Ch 158, 159, 160, 161, 164, 165 and 166, he is named in no document, and he is a man in both passages now. (Five) **Ch 160's third-day entry reported a party that had supplied water between the second and the seventh, four days after the second and on the day the disclosure is made in Ch 163, and it pre-empted that disclosure including the sentence about a figure that is the sum of two things one of which was never measured.** The entry now records only what a man who walked out and back on the third could have brought back, and the sum-that-is-not-a-figure belongs to Ch 163 and is in Ch 163 only.

**Four high findings, all fixed. Ch 167's standing figure said the compost line falls on the first of next month, a Thursday; the first of the eleventh month is day 301 and is a Saturday, and the same sentence in Ch 158 is a Thursday because there it is the first of the tenth month and that is correct.** The two incompatible lists for the count of five are now one list, on the eleventh, the sixteenth, the twenty-first, the twenty-ninth and the thirtieth of the ninth month, in Ch 158 and in Ch 159, and **Ch 158 no longer counts a statement that had not happened when it was written.** Ch 166's third thing was nine miles' road for a holder who is eleven miles off in eight other places in the batch and in the same speech's fifth item. **Ch 163's man of the north row was *thinking about that for nine miles*; the sentence is now *I have been turning it over on the nine miles back*, which is what a man who has just walked nine miles can say.**

**Seven medium findings, all fixed. The question counter now has one label and one arithmetic.** It read *available to a room* at five in Ch 158, *available to a yard* at one and *the first question at two* in Ch 159, and *questions asked in a yard by this body* at three in Ch 160, and *at five* again in Ch 167. **It is now the count of the questions available to a room in this holding and not taken, five at the gate on the thirtieth of the ninth month, six on the first of the tenth, still six on the second because nothing was asked on the second and the fen entered that nobody was asked to answer him, and six at the end of the batch, and Ch 160's entry says the standing of the count out loud: it goes up when a question is put and not when a figure is put on a table.** Ch 166 said *The chapter did not have him answer her*, which is the one place in the batch where the writing refers to itself, and it named Marek Vale in a sentence the volume holds by function. **The paragraph now reads that the engineer of record did not answer her, which is what the entry under it has said all along, and the count of nine seconds is untouched.** Ch 163's closing sentence called the party on the return *the fourth one* against an entry that says the count of the three is three; **the closing sentence now says the count of the not-askings is three, the three are on a page side by side, and the party on a return nine miles off is a fourth of the four and is not on that page and is not going to be put on it.** Ch 163's *four of those days* was four inside a window the return itself defines as the second to the seventh, and the page now says **every one of those six days**, which is what the document says, and documents in this manuscript are correct. Ch 159's *the penny came down a penny by itself* is now *a penny that came down by itself*. Ch 162's *not going to do it a third time in nine days* is now *not going to do it a third time*, the trailing clause being a slip and the summary of the chapter never having carried it. **The room of four was given three dates.** Ch 156 put it on the twenty-fourth of the ninth month, in a seed house with the bay doors shut, and Ch 165 gave it the twenty-fourth of last month in the entry, *a month ago* in Tova Reed's mouth, and *the fourth of a month ago* in the closing paragraph. **It is the twenty-fourth of last month in all three now**, and *a thing that has been in one room for a month* is **seventeen days**, which is the twenty-fourth of the ninth month to the eleventh of the tenth, and the entry under it says seventeen days too. **A fourth date for the same room was found on the pass and is the same defect: Ch 166 had the reader refusing in a yard to tell it *what happened in a room on the eleventh of this month*, and nothing happened in a room on the eleventh of this month, and the eleventh is a day the standing figures were read at a gate and a man of about thirty-five came down a slope. It is the twenty-fourth of last month in the speech and in the entry under it, and the eleventh of last month is where the sentence about a name being a thing other people can spend was said, which is a yard and is a different thing, and the two are not to be run together.**

**The audit disclosure the pass restored.** Batch 0001 withdrew a false *zero duplicates* claim and disclosed its two deliberate repeats. **Batch 0002 made no duplicate claim at all, which is not the same as having none, and there are four sentences of nine words or more that appear twice.** Three of them are the batch's own standing figures and are refrains by design: *the reading of the thirteenth of a month two months back is five-eighths* (Ch 158 and Ch 167), *this body will not say whether the difference is a movement or a season* (Ch 158 and Ch 167), and the whole of *day minus nineteen, unmoved, on a bed at three and a half degrees, both undertakings standing, the drawer was not opened this month and is not to be* (Ch 158 and Ch 167), which is a document read out at two ends of a month and is not a slip. **The fourth is *the run of it is nine months and it has not been discharged in nine*, twice inside Ch 159, and it is disclosed rather than cut: it stands in the steward's book as a line of the ninth month's entry, and it stands again in the clerk's entry at the north gate on the same afternoon, which is the clerk reading a line back out of the book, and a line read back is the same line and not a second line.** No fifth repeat exists, and none of the four is an accident of phrasing.

**Re-measured after the pass.** `wc -w` gives **29,252 words across the ten files, range 2,692–3,117**, and every chapter is inside the 2,600–3,120 per-chapter target and the batch inside 27,000–30,000; **no band was widened and no chapter is declared outside it.** **No non-ASCII glyph in any of the ten files. No paragraph ends with a speech open. The word *seat* is absent, *nest* is absent as a word, the six grep hits are inside *honest*, Brinewake is still spoken once and only once, in Chapter 162, and the blank in the four-line entry in Chapter 167 is still in the book and still unremarked by anybody in the room.** The day-minus figures are unchanged and check: two hundred and four and two hundred and fifty-one at the thirtieth of the ninth month, two hundred and nineteen and two hundred and sixty-six at the fifteenth of the tenth. The four money derivations are unchanged and check: eighty pounds; seventy-eight pounds six shillings and eightpence; a difference of one pound thirteen shillings and fourpence; and one thousand five hundred and twenty hundredweight at sixteen pence against seventy-six pounds paid, a difference of twenty-five pounds six shillings and eightpence, derived again at fourpence a hundredweight and agreeing. **The one new figure in the pass is ninety-five times eleven, which is one thousand and forty-five, and it was checked before it went on the page.**

# VOLUME 04 — BATCH 0003, CHAPTERS 168–177, MOVEMENT 3: THE THING THAT REFUSES

**Ten chapters, days 286–300, the sixteenth through the thirtieth of the tenth month, the whole of the tenth month with NO month turn in it and therefore ONE naming table. The volume's outline is `outline/volume-04.md` and the governing prompt for this batch is `workspace/volume-04/batch-0003/PROMPT.md`. The next prompt is `workspace/volume-04/batch-0004/PROMPT.md`.**

- **168 — *The Sixteenth*** — day 286, the sixteenth, a Friday. **The volume's second clock, read from outside, four hundred miles off, at the fourth hour, in the yard of the fourth house at Cauldron Reach, by a woman of fifty-four with her own nail and her own string, and the figure is a round one and nobody on that shelf writes down what a round figure means.** **THE READING IS FIVE FEET**, said in the plainest words available to her: *Five feet. Not five feet and an inch and not five feet and a half. Five feet, on the string, this morning, at the fourth hour, and the chalk is the same chalk. I have read this well the same way since the flood and I am not going to start reading it a different way because the number has come out round. A round number is still a number that a nail and a string put there, and a man who wants an inch in it is a man who wants a reason on my wall.* And then the reason she is going to say it out loud: **nine feet, and five, and the four in between has been every figure there is, and this morning it is a round one, and I am going to write it on a wall, and I am not going to write what it is.** The **whole series is on the page in wall order** and may not be re-dated — nine feet, five and a half, five five, five four, five two, five three, five and two, five and one, **five feet** — and **under the last line, in the same chalk and the same hand, the only sentence on that wall that is not a figure: I do not write what it is made of. A. Ollvar.** **Nine feet less five feet is four feet, and that is what a person writes on a wall and nothing else, and the fen entered that it is not going to decompose four feet and has not decomposed it, and the half inch of the sixteenth of last month is on the wall and is not carried forward as a figure.** Then the rest of that yard and the rest of that shelf: the grass cut every ten days by the man with the spade; **a place in the stack against the north wall where a thing stood for thirty-one years and does not stand now, and the four of them have not agreed on a name for it and none of the four will say what the thing was**; the first row of eleven faces with two stakes at the two that were not read; **and the older row, which is the row nobody has read, where there is green coming up out of the collars, and three of the four have looked and one has not and said he is not going to, and NOBODY DUG, NOBODY WEIGHED IT, NOBODY NAMED IT, AND NOBODY HAS SAID THAT THE TELLING IS STILL GOING ON AND NOBODY HAS SAID THAT IT HAS STOPPED.** The day letter on its stone with **the eight days from the eighth to the seventeenth of last month still not written on it**; the man with the spade **who has not spoken to the other three since the seventeenth of last month and has not been asked**; and **a fourth line of that yard's own account, new this month, which this fen has not been told, which is that the well came up half an inch in eight days and went down half an inch in a night, and the occupier will write the number on the wall and will not write what the two numbers are.** The fen was four days late and entered that it is four days late and has not written and asked. Then the handoff, four sentences at the north gate, **from a person who is not on that shelf**: the reading is in a book four hundred miles off and not in this one, the first of next month is a Saturday and is not this book's, **and there are two dates and no figure, and a body that has entered a date with nothing against it four times this season did it once on purpose in a room with four people in it and got told what a concealment is worth, and I am not going to enter a fifth one from a gate.** Panel: none. Notes: none.
- **169 — *What A Holder May Do*** — days 287–288, the seventeenth and the eighteenth, a Saturday and a Sunday. **A return comes up the fen road on the ordinary post from the holder of record of a well on the dry road, and it is four lines, and it is correct, and it is the first thing a holder of record has ever sent into this book that was not a schedule.** It states what he is going to do under his own charter, on his own ground, at his own cost, **gives the day and the hour and does not give a figure, and his reason is that a cost entered is a cost that anybody may read**, and **asks no person for any form, no certificate and no answer.** The person who keeps the seed house says what the sheet is standing up, the reader appointed is the man of about fifty, it is read back, the hour goes on it, and **the form on the bench is not looked at by anybody in that yard and is not returned and is not torn up and is not mentioned to him, and the fen entered all four of those and entered the reason for the fourth: a body that has already said it will not put its name to a form and will not refuse to either does not get to change its mind on a Sunday because a piece of paper came in the post, and the difference between not knowing and refusing has been the whole argument of this book for a year.** Then the frame, said once more and said to be the last time: **a charter holder is a supply and is not a thief, and I have said that in this yard three times in a month and I am going to say it a fourth time and then stop, because a thing said four times is a rule and a rule that nobody wrote down is the worst kind. A holder who takes his own well apart and puts it back together at his own cost on his own ground under his own charter has not become anything else by doing it. He has not become a good man. He has not become one of us. He has not done it for this holding and he has not said one word to this holding about it. And I cannot thank him, and I am not going to stand in this yard and pretend to a person in it that not being able to thank a man is the same as not owing him something. Those are two different sentences and I have only got one of them and the one I have got is the first.** **THE RESISTANCE:** about four minutes later one of about eleven people said the word *thank you* out loud about a man nine miles off, and the reader had four seconds to decide in front of eleven people whether this holding corrects its own yard, **and what he decided is that it cannot, because the man did not do it for this holding and a thing a man does to himself is not owed a word by a yard** — and about four people in that yard are owed something by a man nine miles off and this holding cannot say what and will not guess. **The count of the things this holding has been told no by this season stands at six and this is NOT one of them, entered on purpose, because a thing a man does to himself is not a refusal and must not be counted as one.** Then the Sunday, in the seed house with the bay doors shut: **the batch's arithmetic beat, checked twice and agreeing** — one thousand five hundred and twenty hundredweight at one shilling and fourpence, being sixteen pence, is twenty-four thousand three hundred and twenty pence, which is two thousand and twenty-six shillings and eightpence, which is one hundred and one pounds six shillings and eightpence, against seventy-six pounds paid, a difference of twenty-five pounds six shillings and eightpence, **derived a second way as fourpence a hundredweight and agreeing** — and **the fifth thing she cannot do, which is a cost eleven miles off at a man's own cost that he has not entered and is not going to, and the sentence that is the batch's standing position on arithmetic: every one of the four things this holding can compute out of a holder's return is a figure this holding can hand to an office; that is a new and unwanted property of a figure; a figure used to be a complaint and is now a thing that travels; and she does not have a column in this book for a figure she is glad she can check and is not going to build one.** The examiner went through the second derivation a third time with her finger on the paper after saying out loud that she keeps time and does not round, and the reader asked one question and got one answer. Panel: none. Notes: none.
- **170 — *It Came Back On A Tuesday*** — days 289–290, the nineteenth and the twentieth, a Monday and a Tuesday. On the nineteenth the reader asks whether anybody is going down the dry road tomorrow and nobody is, and says the reason out loud: **this holding has no standing on that road, it has had none for eleven years, and a body that turns up at a repair it did not ask about is a body asking to be a party to it, and I am not going to be thanked for that and I am going to be blamed for it by somebody in about four years and that is a price and not a fault.** On the twentieth a **schedule** comes up on the ordinary post, four lines, correct, **a notice and not a return**: the Pans well is running, the licensed draw resumed at about the fourth hour, **the hours of a day are the hours of the charter and not the hours of anybody who is short of water**, the quantity is not on this sheet and will be on the next. **THE LOW QUARTER HAS WATER**, said once and not twice and not to be thanked, and then the half that is in the yard because it has to be: **this holding does not know what that water costs that quarter now and is not going to work it out, because it has done that sum three times in a month and a fourth time would make a number into a thing four hundred and forty-one people argue about, and this holding has no standing to start that and cannot stop it once it has.** Then the instrument, **at about the fourth hour of the same afternoon, at the boards over the shaft in this holding's own low ground, with a licensed man, and the reading of the test written down before the instrument was laid on anything**: the reading of the twenty-first of last month was three and a half, the reading of the twenty-eighth was three and a half, **and the reading of this afternoon is three and seven tenths, and NO DIFFERENCE IS WRITTEN**, in the licensed man's own hand, and his reason: *a difference between two readings taken on two different days with a plain wire gauge is not a number of tenths of an inch, it is two numbers with a day between them.* **THE THING HE WILL NOT SAY IS A PERSON'S NAME, and he said neither half out loud: he will not say the flow under that floor is larger because a man put his own well back together eleven miles off, and he will not say that it is not, because the first of those is a finding and the second of those is a finding and he has got a piece of metal and not a finding — and the instrument is a piece of metal that cannot be checked, and the man who could check it is not in that yard and has said he will not be asked again by this body, and those two are eleven days apart in this book and are not to be read as one.** The mark hot for the rest of the day, the hand no use for about a day, the case carried up the slope by the man from the north row. **No use-log line, entered with the reason before anything else.** The count of the seventh column goes to **thirty-three**, and the compost line is not entered and the reason is entered, and the count of the six days this body has said that out loud is entered and **is not a complaint.** Then the four lines off the four-day road from the fourth house at Cauldron Reach, at that household's own cost, **entered entire, and the reader did not write a fifth line under them and did not write a reason, and entered the reason in one sentence: two numbers that a woman four hundred miles off did not write what they are are not two numbers this holding can make into one, and a body that adds a reason under four correct lines from a yard four hundred miles off is a body that has started editing people.** And at the sixth hour the man of the north row with the cough came back from the low quarter and said what he had found there in about six sentences: nine or ten pails at that row's end of the lane by the sixth hour, more after that, **the men with the pails are not the same men who were on that lane in the first week of the month, and nobody in that quarter said anything to me at all, not good and not bad, and I stood at a gate for about a quarter of an hour and I am used to being told to go away and I was not told to go away once, and I do not know what that means and I am not going to stand in this yard and tell nine people that the water has made a quarter polite, because a quarter with water in it is not a polite quarter, it is a quarter with water in it.** Panel: none. Notes: none.
- **171 — *The Word, Used*** — day 291, the twenty-first, a Wednesday. **The word *nest* is used out loud in this volume for the first time since the man refused it, and it is said once, in the middle of the reading of the fourth line of the standing list, by a person who has read no ground in his life, and the fen entered the use without softening it and entered the function and not the man.** The man of about thirty-five was four feet away and heard it and did not pretend he had not, and about twenty-five people watched a man decide in about two seconds not to pretend. **And the refusal this time is not a definition and is a method, and it is the first time anybody in this book has said what he actually does**: *I have read four pieces of ground. Nineteen years, and not nine, and I said nine to a man on this road in the middle of last month because nine is the answer that makes a man stop asking, and I have been carrying that for a month and I am going to correct it in a yard. Three of the four were crews with a licence and a schedule. And in none of the three did the schedule say that what was being held was being held because somebody was doing work on it every day. The fourth had nobody in it, it is down the coast road, I have not been back, and I am not going to say what I found there. I am not going to tell you what it is and you are not going to get it out of me, and this is the third time I have said that and it is the same answer.* And then the half that is checkable, which he has given twice and gave a third time in front of sixteen people who had not heard it twice: **a nest has one thing in it, and one thing in it has a number, and a thing with a number in it is finished; the thing under that floor is not finished and it does not stop; it does not go where the ground goes; it is doing work, and the work is not water and it is not seeds, and it is the sort of work that stops if nobody does it** — and: *it has grown since the twenty-second of last month and I did not have anything to do with that and I was not asked and I came and said it anyway, and the pail you gave me has not come back and I have not been paid, and I am not asking, and I am not going to be thanked.* One of the twenty-five asked him afterwards what the word was and he said no in one word, **and the count of that is three.** Then the one question nobody else in that yard could ask, with the reason first: **is the refusal about the word, or is it about the counting — because a man who will not name a thing may be protecting that thing or may be protecting himself, and this holding has to know which of those before it writes his refusal down in its own book as a virtue.** And the answer: **Both, and I will not separate them for you, and you may write down that I would not separate them, which is the only part of it that is about me.** Then the fourth man in that yard, who had come up the fen road on foot and had not come for the reading, and **said who he was before he said anything else: I am nobody's guide and I am not here as a representative of anything and I have no paper** — and named **the Assemblies of the Thornwild** for the first time in this volume, none of them a rank, none ever given one, he has been in two and been turned out of one, **because about four years ago a body like this one would have written down the word wild and left it at that** — and refused the same word on a different ground: *I have never read one.
