and there is a second man with a cart in that lane, he is not the man with the cart, he has been on that lane four days, he does not come from this district, and nobody knows where his water comes from and the man did not ask, and the fen entered that he did not ask and entered the reason, which is the same one as last time: it is not my row.** Nobody in that yard said anything about the second cart, and the fen entered that he said a man was on a lane and did not say a word about how he got the water, and that those two halves are in this book and are not to be joined up by anybody in four years. **The second, smaller, in four sentences: the household that has been counting its own pails at a gate for eleven years was told on the fourth of this month, by the officer with the book, in a yard of about twenty-five people, that a gate count is not a record; he was not being cruel, he was doing his business, it is not one of the four lines on his sheet and it is not wrong, and it is going to be in a room somewhere in about four years and this holding has entered it and does not know where; and that household kept the count anyway, every day, and on the morning of the fourteenth the woman of that household got up at the fourth hour and walked up this road with nine years of it in a sheet in her own hand, one line a month, and stood in this yard at about the seventh hour and gave it to the clerk and said she would like it entered, and it is entered, and she did not want anything else and she did not stay; and she counted pails for eleven years for her own house and counted them again on that morning for a body she is not part of and has never been asked to be, and nobody in that yard asked her to, and the count is on the page and it is in her hand and not in anybody else's.** A line of it: **a date at the left, a number at the right, nothing in between, the numbers all three figures or all two, and the hand does not change in nine years except once, in the fourth year, where a five has been made into a three and the three made over the five, and there is no note, and the woman said the fifth one is the one she is not sure of and she is leaving it as it is.** **The batch's second sum, said out loud with the bay doors shut on the Friday night, not asked for, with the word before the figure: I AM GOING TO DO A SUM I DID NOT WANT TO DO, AND I HAVE NEVER IN MY LIFE NOT WANTED A SUM, SO I AM GOING TO SAY THAT OUT LOUD FIRST, AND I AM GOING TO DO IT BECAUSE A SUM IS FOR DOING AND NOT FOR WANTING. Two hundred and fourteen pails for a month at sixteen pence the hundredweight, being three thousand four hundred and twenty-four pence, derived twice and agreeing — two hundred and fourteen at a shilling is two thousand five hundred and sixty-eight pence and two hundred and fourteen at fourpence is eight hundred and fifty-six, and the two come to three thousand four hundred and twenty-four; three thousand four hundred and twenty-four pence is two hundred and eighty-five shillings and four pence, which is fourteen pounds five shillings and fourpence. And the part she did not want, said in a room and not in a yard: that figure is the only number on that sheet, that sheet is eleven years of a household counting its own water at its own gate in its own hand, and this holding has taken four words off the top of it and turned it into a rate this holding can hand to an office; that household asked for the count to be entered and did not ask for it to be priced; I have priced it; nobody in that room stopped me; the woman who keeps the money did not say one word about it while I did it and said one word about it afterward. ANNEKE ROOS, WHO IS THE WOMAN WHO DOES THE SUMS, SAID THE WORD. SHE SAID NOW. She said it once and did not elaborate and was not thanked and did not ask to be, and the fen entered that the woman who priced it said the word and that nobody in that room could have stopped her and that she did not need stopping and that the two of those facts are on the same line.** **Then the sentence that is the chapter: A FIGURE THAT A BODY CANNOT CHECK IS NOT A PROTECTION. IT IS A DEBT. And this holding has had those four for a month and this month we found out whether a form helps, and a form does not help. They are the same four, named: what that quarter drinks now; the strip about two paces wide in front of four houses and what comes up out of it; the eleven a man gave in a yard on the twenty-seventh of last month, which is a number of the ones who came to a decision and not a number of anybody; and what a man spent on his own ground eleven miles off and has not written down and is not going to. And they are not one more than they were on the first of this month and not one fewer, and the count of four figures this holding cannot check and the count of the four things this body does not know are two different counts and are still not to be run together, and she has said that twice this season and is saying it a third time.** **Then the fault line, out loud, at about the eleventh hour on the Saturday in a yard of about nine, with the standing rule read out in full before the figures were closed and the count of the readings in this month entered at four and the count of the uses at two and the two counts declared to be two counts: THE FAULT LINE IN THIS BODY USED TO BE BETWEEN TWO PEOPLE. THEN IT WAS BETWEEN A BODY AND A JOB. AND IT IS NOW BETWEEN A BODY AND A CLASS, and the reason the third is worse is that two people can refuse each other, a body can refuse a job and we have watched that happen and it cost us a woman and a book and it was survivable, AND A CLASS CANNOT BE REFUSED — a class is a thing an office puts on a form, and this body cannot get out of one by refusing it, because refusing a form is not a thing anybody can do twice. And he put this holding in that class in his own hand on the fourth of this month and would do it again, and the reason he would is that the alternative was being put in it by somebody else, and he has never worked out which of the two he would rather have and has had a month to try.** Panel: none. Notes: none.
- **187 — *The Twentieth*** — days 316–320, the sixteenth through the twentieth, a Sunday through a Thursday. **The twentieth, a Thursday, is the last working day this batch owns, and at about the eleventh hour the standing figures are read out whole in a yard of about nine with nothing softened.** The compost line for the tenth month at eighty pounds on four hundred hundredweight at four shillings, derived twice and agreeing, the ninth month's figure also eighty pounds and the two on the same line, **the run at ten not discharged in ten, a figure that repeats entered as a worse kind of figure than a figure that moves, the word crowded, and the word and the figure not agreeing.** **The compost line for the eleventh month NOT ENTERED with the reason entered, the figure falling on the first of next month, which is a Saturday, and the eleventh month of a run of eleven not discharged in eleven once that figure is in.** The leak at three-quarters of an inch, read on the twenty-first of a month two months back, no verdict on the difference from the thirteenth of a month three months back, not measured since, not to be measured again. **The beat not logged since, cause not established, column open, the column not opened in front of nine people and the not opening entered as a decision and not an oversight, and the fen entered that the day of that beat is not restated in this book and is not going to be.** **The seventh column at thirty-five, the last reading day of this month the thirtieth, which is not this batch's, and the count has stood at thirty-five since the first because the door is read on the first and the thirtieth of a month and has been for four years.** **The boards at two hundred and fifty-four days, day minus sixty-six, and the March's tin at three hundred and one days, day minus nineteen, both read out loud by the clerk before either number, which he has now done on nine reading days running and which nobody has asked for on any of the nine.** **The standing rule four lines, satisfied on the third of a month five months back, used twice in this volume, on the twenty-second of a month two months back and on the thirteenth of this month, count of the uses two and not going to be three.** The use log at fifteen lines, the fifteenth a contact on a boarded floor on the ninth of last month, **and entered on the same standing entry and not in the log, because none of them is a line: a second contact on the twentieth of last month, a hold on the same boards on the twenty-fourth of last month, and a draw on the thirteenth of this month.** **The rootmark four inches, branching twice, unchanged, no second mark, the burn not a mark, and four accounts of that arm none of which agrees with any of the others, and the draw of the thirteenth is not a fifth account and reconciles none of them.** The form on a bench, correct, filled in, not returned, not torn up, answer not given. **The well on the shelf at five feet on the sixteenth of last month, four feet against the sixteenth of a month six months back, not decomposed, and no entry anywhere for that well coming back, with the day letter, the man with a spade, and the fourth line with the two numbers this body did not add up.** **Then the standing rule read out again in full, four lines, in the same yard, before the figures were closed, because a rule read only when it is used is a habit and this one is not a habit — and the fen entered that it has now been read out in full five times in this month and used twice, and that the two counts are two counts and are not the same count, and entered that the rule says nothing whatever about what comes after the stop, and that on the thirteenth nine people came up a slope and a road did not open, and every one of those four lines was satisfied the whole time and not one of them was about the nine.** Then the change, read out because a month of standing is not closed by a column: **the seed side is on a road, the water is on a form, a man eleven miles off is a supply and not a thief and has been said in that yard four times and will not be said a fifth, a man of about thirty-five will not say a name and has refused four times in nineteen years, a thing under a low quarter will not be drawn from and eleven came up a road and said so in a yard and gave a figure that is not a number of anybody; a party has become a person, and the person said a true thing about a form and a quarter that this holding has spent a year arguing against and cannot answer one word of, and said the method out loud after the true thing and not before it, and he was not thanked and nobody was forgiven anything; and this holding has four figures it cannot check and a form it has not answered and a cart it will not ask about, and every one of those is entered and none of them is thanked, and he has come to the end of that list and is not going to add a fifth thing to it in a yard to see how it reads.** **Then the man of about seventy is fetched at about the fourth hour, the fifteenth time in a season, given the figures and the counts in that order, asked for the last one to be repeated, and says four words: ASK WHO COUNTS THEM.** Nobody answered him and nobody asked him what it was about, **and the fen entered that it does not know and is not going to write and ask, and entered that in fifteen times it has never twice been about the same thing.** Then the handoff at the north gate at about the seventh hour, in about nine people, four sentences: **the first of next month is a Saturday, and it is the day the compost line for the eleventh month falls on, and this holding does not know what that figure is, and the door has a column and a month and a figure, and this body has no standing on any of the three; it did not write and ask and is not going to, and the fen entered the day and not the figure, the way it entered the day and not the figure for a shelf four hundred miles off; and that is the third clock this body owns that it can count and cannot price, and he is not going to make a column out of the three of them, and is not going to say the word pattern out loud in this yard about any of them, and the clerk entered that he did not.** Nobody so much as one word was said. **And the forty-eighth note under section nine, dated the twentieth, signed by nobody but Marek, read back before it was initialled, there is no charter, and the note says so on its face, carrying the standing sentence: THIS IS WHAT A MONTH OF A VOLUME IS.** Panel: none. Notes: the **forty-eighth**, dated **the twentieth**, **carrying the standing sentence.**

**The batch is 28,214 words by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings, range 2,662–3,114, inside the outline's 27,000–30,000, and every one of the ten is inside the outline's 2,600–3,120 per-chapter target at ten of the ten. The band was NOT widened and no chapter is declared outside it.** The ten figures are **2,892 + 2,731 + 2,705 + 2,744 + 2,662 + 2,773 + 2,718 + 3,087 + 2,788 + 3,114.** Ch 182 at 2,662 is the shortest and is filled with a day and an hour given in advance, the ordinary business of a Saturday carrying on around an apology, a room with a date, an hour and a number of people in it, two readings of a page in the ordinary voice, one sentence of an answer, a refusal to say it twice, and a question nobody took, and not with summary. Ch 187 at 3,114 is the longest, carries the movement's second clock, the standing figures of a month, the standing rule read out in full a fifth time, the fifteenth fetching of the man of about seventy and the forty-eighth section-nine note, and **may not be lengthened.** *(All ten figures and the batch total were recomputed after the review repair pass; the batch stood at 28,202 before it. See `state/continuity.md`,* Fixes applied to the drafted Batch 0004 on review*.)* Mechanical check: **no paragraph ends with its speech open in any of the ten files**; **the number of marks in a file is not a test of anything in this manuscript and was not made even**; **no non-ASCII glyph at all**, so no curly mark and no em dash; **no month named** in narration or in any document — the rule is the outline's, a day is an ordinal, *of this month* or *of last month*, a day of the first month is a bare number only, a named month is not carried in a distance, and the ordinal distances (*a month six months back*, *a month two months back*, *the sixteenth of last month*) are the house idiom and are used throughout — and the only capitalised *March* being *the March's tin*, in Chs 178, 181, 182 and 187. **The word *seat* does not appear. The word *nest* does not appear. Thornwild and Assemblies are not named, and the permission for both remains unspent.** No Crown Engine, no Iona Vey, no False Season, no Quiet Break, no Continuity Office, no Halden Pike, no seedheart, no Glassward, no Assemtry, no Seren Quill in the batch. **The name *Brinewake* is nowhere in the batch and the four-line entry is not read out in it, so the blank in that entry is untouched and is not an omission.** No romance and nothing tender on the page. **The arithmetic was checked line by line and it holds:** four hundred hundredweight at four shillings is one thousand six hundred shillings being eighty pounds, and at forty-eight pence is nineteen thousand two hundred pence being two hundred and forty pence eighty times over; one thousand and forty-five hundredweight at sixteen pence is sixteen thousand seven hundred and twenty pence being sixty-nine pounds thirteen shillings and fourpence against fifty-two pounds five, a difference of seventeen pounds eight shillings and fourpence being four thousand one hundred and eighty pence, and four thousand one hundred and eighty on one thousand and forty-five is fourpence; one licensed-man day at one pound is twenty shillings, and four pounds five less one pound is sixty-five shillings being three pounds and five shillings, and in pence a thousand and twenty less two hundred and forty is seven hundred and eighty, which is the same; two hundred and fourteen at sixteen pence is three thousand four hundred and twenty-four pence being two hundred and eighty-five shillings and fourpence being fourteen pounds five shillings and fourpence, and at a shilling plus fourpence the two come to the same. **The boards and the tin are the day-minus rules and are true at two hundred and thirty-five and two hundred and eighty-two on day 301 and at two hundred and fifty-four and three hundred and one on day 320.** **Ten locks respected and every one of them is checkable on the page**, and **twelve recorded deviations from the batch's own cards are set out in `state/continuity.md`, all deliberate and all checkable.** Volume 01 stands at 166,022 words, Volume 02 at 141,818, Volume 03 at 142,952, Volume 04 Batches 0001, 0002 and 0003 at 28,503, 29,252 and 28,472, so the manuscript stands at **565,234 words across 187 chapters**, recomputed from the files and reconciling exactly.

## Volume 04 Batch 0005 — Chapters 188–196, Movement 5, *The Long Pan* (drafted)

- **188 — *The Wrong Day In The Book*** — days 321–323, the twenty-first, the twenty-second, the twenty-third, a Friday, a Saturday, a Sunday. **The reader of this body reads this holding's own book at about the third hour on the twenty-first and finds that he has been keeping a date four hundred miles off out of his own head for eleven years while a certified copy of the schedule of readings lay on the seed house table the whole time; and then, shortly before the fourth hour, in a yard of about eleven people, he reads out that the next ordinary reading is on the first of next month, which his own hand calls a Saturday. GAEL SENN, the examiner from the district, is a woman who keeps time, and she says out loud, before anything is written down, that the first of next month is a Monday and not a Saturday and that she keeps time and does not round, and the day number is the first of next month and is not in question. The day number is wrong too, because the well on that shelf is read on the sixteenth of every month and not on the first of any month.** Both figures are entered, neither is improved on, and the count of the two days this book has got wrong in its own hand is now two and is not reduced. The tenth reading was taken four hundred miles off on the sixteenth, at the fourth hour, by the occupier, with her own nail, in her own yard, on her own wall, and **this holding has no standing on that ground, did not write and ask, and is not going to, and the figure is not on this page and is not going to be.** The four lines of the standing rule went down that road at that household's own cost and arrived on or about the twentieth. **The count of three day-and-not-the-figure entries stands at three and does not come down, and the fen enters that one of the three was aimed at a day that was not a reading day at all and that this is not an improvement on the other two.**
- **189 — *A True And Narrow Thing*** — days 324–326, the twenty-fourth, the twenty-fifth, the twenty-sixth, a Monday, a Tuesday, a Wednesday. **The form on the bench in the seed house is answered in the SECOND box and not the first.** The first box asks whether a supply is broken; the second asks what a body did about it and in what quantity. **It is a true and narrow thing and the fen enters that the narrowness is the whole of what he has this month.** Returned with the counter's mark, the hour and the name of the clerk who copied it, and the original never returned and never torn up and on the bench since **the twenty-seventh of last month**, and the copy cost four pence a sheet at the engineer of record's own cost. **Consequence, entered the same day: an office in the ordinary course reduced the licensed draw at the Pans well by a fifth on the twenty-sixth, and the reduction is correct, and the holding did not appeal it and did not ask why.**
- **190 — *Three Places And Three Numbers*** — days 327–329, the twenty-seventh, the twenty-eighth, the twenty-ninth, a Thursday, a Friday, a Saturday. One pail, one stopwatch, one rod, one line, three places, three numbers: **one hundred and forty-six seconds, two hundred and two, one hundred and eighty-eight — the third faster than the second, and nobody improved on it.** The plan drawn on the twenty-ninth of a month two months back at the ninth hour of the evening, without standing on the ground, is wrong in its second half mile and is south of where it should be. **A rise of eleven feet in two hundred yards, which is about one in fifty-five, and the chapter says out loud that a grade of that slope is walkable for two hundred yards and not for a mile and a half.** A trial channel four feet long held an inch for an hour and a half. **The corridor's own volume is put down as a number and derived twice, the two agreeing: nine hundred yards by forty at two inches is two thousand cubic yards, which is three million three hundred and seventy-five thousand pounds, which is about thirty thousand hundredweight — and the comparison with the ninety-five a day is refused, and the refusal is the beat.** A gate is opened by a licensed man under a form and **is not a draw**, and the use log gets no line, and the fen says why out loud: a line records a contact and never a reading.
- **191 — *A Constant Times A Constant*** — days 330–332, the thirtieth, the first, the second, a Sunday, a Monday, a Tuesday. **The month turns overnight and the eleventh month's compost line falls on the first, at about the eleventh hour, in a yard of about twenty-five — which is the only day its own notice allows.** Four hundred hundredweight at four shillings is one thousand six hundred shillings, which is eighty pounds, derived twice out loud and the two agreeing, the second derivation at forty-eight pence a hundredweight being nineteen thousand two hundred pence, being two hundred and forty pence eighty times over. **It is the third consecutive month of eighty pounds and the run is eleven not discharged in eleven.** The word before the figure was *crowded*, and **a constant multiplied by a constant is not a measurement of anything outside that door**, and the figure and the word have never disagreed and are not going to be made to. The seventh column is read **thirty-six on the thirtieth and thirty-seven on the first, a day apart**, and the rule is not changed. The standing rule is read out in full in that same yard before the figures are closed — **six times across two months, five of them in the last month and this one in this one** — and its **count of uses is two and is not going to be three.** The month's costs go on four separate lines and are not folded into the shortfall. **The woman who does the sums says *crowded* again and this time says what it means: the figure has been the same for three months because the specification has not changed and the valuation has not changed, so the eighty pounds is a constant multiplied by a constant, and the word and the figure never disagreed because they were never about the same thing, and she is not glad about it, and a debt that nobody else owes is a debt this holding invented, and she says she was able this morning, and that it is thirty days from the first of last month, and that she is not going to stand in a yard and say that thirty days is quick.** The holding itself reads the third line as though it said *in a yard*, and the notice does not say it.
- **192 — *The Water Goes Where The Ground Says*** — days 333–335, the third, the fourth, the fifth, a Wednesday, a Thursday, a Friday. **The climax.** At about the fourth hour on the fourth, a licensed man opens the head of this holding's own ground under a form and a rate, writes the hour and the figure in his own book, and the fen stands at the top of the slope and does not go down. Four people carry water into the pan in pails and cut a channel and stand over the crew, which is a thing this holding has no custom for. **The corridor the sheet describes is three hundred yards south of where it was drawn, and the fen measured it himself on the plan and got the same three hundred yards, and the page has the hour on it and the hour is his own.** The rise is one in fifty-five and the ground goes the other way from the drawing. **A hold for four was put on it and the count of people who can hold a pattern for four is one and is public and does not change.** Coll Renner says the professional thing once, to the four men and to the engineer of record and to nobody else, before he packs the case.
- **193 — *Roald Trench And Sena Wick*** — days 336–339, the sixth, the seventh, the eighth, the ninth, a Saturday, a Sunday, a Monday, a Tuesday. **A stone-and-turf seed vault of forty households, four hundred yards off the north edge of the pan, built at their own cost in a month three months back, with no holder and no charter and no form on which anybody can be asked about it, on ground the district calls Crown-held and has never held, goes under at about the seventh hour of the ninth.** **ROALD TRENCH, of about forty-four years, and SENA WICK, of about thirty-eight years, are named out of a woman's hand and neither is of this holding and neither is a member of anything.** The vault is not recovered. **This holding was not there and did not ask and has not been asked, and a thing in a woman's hand about the north side is not in this book and this holding did not ask for it.** **The corridor is three hundred yards on the fourth, three hundred and eighty-four at about the eleventh hour on the eighth — sixteen short of the vault — and four hundred and eighty-six at about the seventh hour of the evening on the ninth, and the two speeds are derivable twice from those figures: eighty-four yards in the four days from Thursday to the Monday is twenty-one yards a day, and a hundred and two yards in the thirty-two hours from the Monday at the eleventh hour to the Tuesday at the seventh hour of the evening is seventy-six and a half yards a day, and the reason it went at twenty-one yards a day and then at seventy-six and a half is not established, and the fen says he does not know which of those two numbers is the water and which of them is the ground and is not going to guess at it in a yard.** A man of about thirty-five, **Soren Rill**, comes up the dry road on foot on the ninth, is announced in a yard before he speaks, gives his name and his position and the one thing only he can say about that ground, and does not stay, is not paid and is not thanked. **The man of the north row with the cough comes up the road at about the ninth hour of the evening of the ninth with about two hundred people behind him and says that a thing is in a woman's hand and not in this book, and is not asked for it, and does not give it up.**
- **194 — *Soldiers On The Dry Road*** — days 341–343, the eleventh, the twelfth, the thirteenth, a Thursday, a Friday, a Saturday. **Four soldiers and a man with a schedule walk the corridor that four hundred and eleven people crossed on the fourth.** The road is held from the fourth hour to the fourth hour of the day after tomorrow, the holding of it is in the schedule and is not discretionary, the class is **migration** and the rate is **fourpence the hundredweight**, and the office asks a party for the figure of the goods carried in the last six days and says the figure is not known. The arithmetic is done out loud in a yard: six hundred hundredweight at fourpence is two thousand four hundred pence, which is ten pounds; at sixteen pence it is forty pounds; **the difference is seven thousand two hundred pence, which is thirty pounds a week, and the thirty pounds is what the two prices are arguing about and not what anybody earns.** **The officer of the district says ten pounds and is right, and no office in this volume is anything but correct. The woman of the sums says twenty-four hundred pence is two pounds, stops in the rain, and says it out loud that she is wrong and that the officer said ten pounds before she did and did not look at her when she got it wrong, and an office does not put a party right on a question the party has not asked it. The count of wrong sums said in public is two and the first was eight days in a month at a gate.** The thirty pounds a week is a shilling on every hundredweight anybody moves over that corridor. **The office says it will ask a party for a figure it does not have, because that is what a form is for, and that a party which does not have the figure writes down that it does not have it, and that that is a complete answer and it is not a refusal and it is not a finding. The man of about fifty writes it, the officer reads it back, nobody is charged with anything, and the road opens by the seventh hour.**
- **195 — *Four Lines And A Refusal*** — days 344–346, the fourteenth, the fifteenth, the sixteenth, a Sunday, a Monday, a Tuesday. **A cross-region seed agreement on the trust's own paper, in the seedwright's own hand, in a yard of about twenty-five, read out and read back with the hour on it, four lines, and nothing in it is a finding against any person.** The third clause carries **a *seat* for a body of one and one body of two and a *seat* is not a licence to hold a pattern for four and is not a Stage and is not a body.** **The refusal clause names the cost out loud: Thornwild and the Assemblies, and both words are in the document and are the price of the seed, and the fen does not pretend the price was not named.** **Four hundred at a shilling and fourpence is sixteen pence, is six thousand four hundred pence, which is twenty-six pounds thirteen shillings and fourpence; four hundred at fourpence is six pounds thirteen shillings and fourpence; the difference is exactly twenty pounds, and the exactness is the whole of what is being said, and it is a carriage rate on a season and not a supply and not a wage and not anybody's living.** **A figure this holding computed has come out better than the same figure cost in the third of last month, for the first time, and the woman who does the sums says she does not know what to do with it and is not going to find out this month.** A low quarter states **four hundred and forty-one** in its own book, in its own hand, on its own page. **The eleventh reading of the well on the shelf is taken at the fourth hour on the sixteenth, a Tuesday, by the occupier, with her own nail, in her own yard, on her own wall, in wall order, and it is FIVE FEET. THE FOUR LINES GO DOWN THE FOUR-DAY ROAD AT THAT HOUSEHOLD'S OWN COST ON THAT DAY AND THIS HOLDING DOES NOT HAVE THEM YET, AND THE ENTRY IS OF WHAT HAS HAPPENED AND WHAT HAS NOT. IT DOES NOT HAVE THE TENTH, AND THE OCCUPIER REFUSES TO WRITE IT INTO A SHEET IN HER OWN HAND, BECAUSE A BODY FOUR HUNDRED MILES OFF THAT HAS SPENT A MONTH FINDING OUT IT WAS AIMING AT THE WRONG DAY IS NOT GOING TO BE HANDED A NUMBER BY A WOMAN IT HAS NEVER MET, AND THE WALL SHOWS THE GAP, AND THE WALL READS *AND THEN THE ONE I AM NOT WRITING, AND THEN THIS ONE*. The chapter ends on the sixteenth with the sheet on the road and the figure not in this building, and the reader says *four days*, and the road is four up and four down, which is eight. THE READING-OUT, THE CLERK'S ENTRY AND *Five feet and five feet* ARE NOT IN THIS CHAPTER: THE FIGURE ENTERS THIS HOLDING'S BOOK ON THE TWENTIETH OF THE TWELFTH MONTH, A SATURDAY, AT ABOUT THE NINTH HOUR OF THE MORNING, IN CH 196, WHERE IT BELONGS. There is a month between the two figures it has that is on no line in this book, and nobody in that yard wrote and asked for it, and there is still no entry anywhere for that well coming back.**
- **196 — *The Bottleneck, Said Twice*** — days 347–350, the seventeenth, the eighteenth, the nineteenth, the twentieth, a Wednesday, a Thursday, a Friday, a Saturday. **The seedwright's condition is paid, and it is paid exactly.** She set it in a yard on the twenty-sixth of a month two months back and refused to name the person; **the person was named on the fifteenth of this month by the woman of the low acre, and she named him to the man of about fifty and not to the fen.** He is the man of the north row with the cough, about sixty-eight. He was asked twice and said no once and yes once. **A hold for four was made and put in the book, so the count of people who can hold a pattern for four is TWO and the fen cannot do the second thing and does not know who can, and neither of them knows it is a count of people and not of skill.** The schedule and the rule are written out without Marek, because he was not in the room and nobody sent for him. **The question this book has carried goes into the book unanswered: that question is available and not taken twice and asked once, and the standing count of the questions available to a room in this holding and not taken goes from six to seven, and neither is added to the other, and the fen says there are three counts and that nobody in this book has ever added one of them to another.** **AT ABOUT THE NINTH HOUR OF THE MORNING OF THE TWENTIETH, A SATURDAY, THE FOUR LINES COME DOWN THE FOUR-DAY ROAD IN A LEATHER BAG BY THE ORDINARY POST, WRITTEN AT THE FOURTH HOUR OF THE MORNING OF THE SIXTEENTH AT THAT HOUSEHOLD'S OWN COST, AND THE READER OF THIS BODY READS THE WHOLE OF IT OUT IN A YARD OF ABOUT NINE, THE MAN OF ABOUT FIFTY IS APPOINTED READER, IT IS READ BACK, THE HOUR GOES ON IT, AND THE READER SAYS *Five feet and five feet* AND SAYS WHICH TWO: the ninth was five feet on the sixteenth of a month two months back and the eleventh is five feet on the sixteenth of this month, and there is a month between those two on no line in this book, and the two figures it does have are the same figure, and a figure which repeats is a worse kind of figure than a figure which moves.** The standing figures are read at about the eleventh hour of the twentieth in a yard of about nine with nothing softened. **The standing rule is read out in full for the sixth time and its count of uses is two.** A man of about seventy is fetched for the **sixteenth** time and **says nothing at all.** The bottleneck sentence is said **twice, on purpose, and the fen says out loud that he is saying it again and why.** The clerk makes the one observation this volume spends on a person: **the corridor this holding built is three hundred yards from the corridor this holding drew, the plan is on the page with the hour on it, the road it made is not a road, the vault that went under was four hundred yards from a door this holding did not open, and the reader of this body has not written down what any of that means, and this holding has been arguing since the first month of this book that a thing on a page is a thing somebody can check, and it has not checked this.** **The forty-ninth section-nine note is entered, and the number of entries that ask for nothing and wait for a year is forty-nine and that is a year and a day and it is not going to be rounded.**

**The batch is 27,280 words over nine chapter files including their chapter headings, mean 3,031, range 2,607–4,440, after a third pass that applied the Batch 0005 review and closed a four-day-road regression. It is NINE chapters, not ten, because Ch 196 is the last chapter of Volume 04, and the outline's 27,000–30,000 batch range is a ten-chapter range: nine chapters at the batch mean would have fallen under it, and this pass brought the batch to 27,280 — INSIDE the range for the first time — by repairing five figures and writing one new closing beat in Ch 195, and not by padding a sentence or widening a band. SEVEN OF THE NINE ARE INSIDE THE 2,600–3,120 PER-CHAPTER TARGET. CH 190 AT 3,126 AND CH 196 AT 4,440 ARE DECLARED OUTSIDE IT, 6 AND 1,320 OVER THE CEILING, AND THE BAND IS NOT WIDENED TO COVER EITHER. Ch 196 is the volume's last chapter and it carries thirteen load-bearing standing-figure entries each with a new-this-month clause, the arrival of the eleventh figure of the shelf well, the standing rule read out in full, the sixteenth fetching of the man of about seventy, the payment of the seedwright's condition, the question entering the book, the standing count of questions going from six to seven, the forty-ninth note, the spent clerk's observation, and the handoff. **CH 196 ALSO CARRIES THE THREE `Entered ... by the clerk` BLOCKS IT CARRIED BEFORE AND NOT A FOURTH: the shelf-well arrival is entered inside the fen's standing-figures ledger, which is not the clerk's, precisely to hold the three-block limit on the volume's last chapter.** Ch 190 went six words over the ceiling because the second pass restored the three places the pail went into and the examiner's own line about keeping time, which the first pass had compressed, and the third pass changed *the two places* to *the first two of them*. Ch 195 fell to 2,963 and is inside the band. The two other Volume 04 exceptions are Ch 157 at 3,193 and Ch 151 at 2,599. All four are declared rather than hidden, and the close phase must judge them. Volume 04 is 141,722 over five batches (28,504 + 29,252 + 28,472 + 28,214 + 27,280) and the manuscript is 592,514 over 196 chapters.**

# VOLUME 04 — THE CLOSE, CHAPTERS 148–196: WHAT THE CLOSE CHANGED ON THE PAGE

**The close audited all forty-nine chapters, recomputed every figure from the files, and repaired sixty-four lines in thirty-five of the forty-nine chapters. It wrote no new prose, added no sentence to reach a word count, replaced no scene and moved no beat. THE CHAPTER SUMMARIES ABOVE ARE THE CHAPTERS' SUMMARIES AND THEY STAND; WHAT FOLLOWS IS ONE LINE PER CHAPTER THE CLOSE TOUCHED, WITH THE LINE NUMBERS OF THE CHAPTER AS IT NOW STANDS, AND NOTHING FOR THE CHAPTERS IT DID NOT TOUCH. THE FINDINGS FILE IS `reviews/volume-04.findings.md` AND THE FULL REPAIR LIST, IN ITS OWN NUMBERING, IS IN `state/continuity.md` UNDER *VOLUME 04 — THE CLOSE*.**

**Batch 0001, six chapters touched.** **Ch 150:49** — `thirty` of our own households on this head is **sixteen**, which is the same sentence's own figure and the holding's count everywhere else. **Ch 152:7** — *the only thing in this chapter that makes the other two things mean anything* becomes **in this day's work**, the narrator's first sweep site. **Ch 153:47** — *the reason the whole of this volume is about* becomes **this season is about**, which is the diegetic frame and not a book-keeping one. **Ch 154:55** — `thirty` households on the head is **sixteen**; **:77** — the standing rule's satisfaction, two months back, is **three months back**; **:84** — *used once, on the twelfth of a month two months back* becomes ***the fourteenth of last month***, and this is the repair that **removed a third asserted use of a rule whose volume ledger says the count is two and is not going to be three**. **Ch 155:17** — *at about the fifth hour and forty minutes* becomes **the fourth hour and forty minutes**, against a draw begun at four twenty and the clerk's own entry at :23; eighty minutes was never on the page. **:87** — *I have read ground for nineteen years* becomes **nine years**, the outline's figure for reading ground and the only family in which nineteen was ever a slip. **Ch 156:19, :21, :91** — see the fix pass below for :19; :21 is the second of the two genuine *read ground* sites; **:91** was a bare `""` on a line of its own, the only such line in forty-nine files, and is deleted.

**Batch 0002, eight chapters touched.** **Ch 158:47** — the rule's satisfaction, four months back, is **three months back**; **:85** — *on the eighteenth of last month* is **this month**, the form having come from the sub-office on the eighteenth of the ninth month and the chapter closing that month on the thirtieth. **Ch 160:39** — *Fourpence a hundredweight is the holder's* is **carriage**, against the office's own allocation at Ch 153:11 and Ch 153:29, because no office lies in this book; **:43, :59** — *the half of the chapter* becomes **the half of the day** and *the sentence this chapter is for* becomes **this day is for**. **Ch 162:29** — *a fact about this volume* becomes **a fact about this book's own page**, the one replacement in the sweep that points at a page the fen controls rather than at a frame. **Ch 163:21** — *the second half is the whole of the chapter* becomes **the whole of the day**; the chapter's four clerk's blocks against a limit of three, and its backwards scene order, are **recorded open and not repaired**. **Ch 164:43, :84** — *the thing is the chapter* and *it is the whole of the chapter* become **the day** and **the whole of the day**. **Ch 165:37** — *said the thing the chapter is for* becomes **the day is for**. **Ch 166:46** — *the thing the chapter is for* becomes **the day is for**; its four blocks are recorded. **Ch 167:83** — *nine days of a volume* becomes **nine days of this book**; its four blocks are recorded, **and the blank in its four-line entry is untouched, undescribed and unnoticed, as the volume's lock requires.**

**Batch 0003, six chapters touched.** **Ch 169:25** — *the volume's frame needs* becomes **this holding's frame needs**; the capital *May* at :81 is the modal and stands. **Ch 170:57** — *the eleventh of this month and this afternoon* is **the ninth**, which is what makes the eleven days the chapter's own opening states. **Ch 174:73** — *a bench he had got up off four days ago* is **two days ago**; **:77, :85** — the rule's authorship is **two months back** and not four, at both the clerk's entry and the day's close. **Ch 175:69** — *on the twenty-first, four days ago* is **six days ago**, the chapter being the twenty-seventh. **Ch 176:63** — the fourth cart is **two months back** and not seven. **Ch 177:48** — the rule's authorship is **two months back** and not four; **:86** — the fourth cart is **two months back** and not seven; **:88** — *this is what a month of a volume is* is **a month of this book is**, which is the holding's own book on the middle of the table and therefore diegetic.

**Batch 0004, nine chapters touched.** **Ch 179:11** — *the frame this volume has been standing in for two months* is **this holding's**; **:73** — *the same sentence as the fourth line of a return a month ago* is **the third line**, against Ch 179:27 and against the return itself. **Ch 180:39** — *the thing the chapter is for* becomes **the day is for**. **Ch 181:11** — ***a wet October* is the only month name in forty-nine chapters and is gone**, becoming *a wet month two months back*, the volume's own ordinal idiom, the sack having been in the shed since the twenty-ninth of the seventh month, which is where the season's damp is being described from. **Ch 182:53** — *used once in this volume and not used this month* is **used once this season**. **Ch 183:61, :65** — *the whole argument of this volume* is **this season**, at the yard and in the clerk's entry. **Ch 184:49** — *the sentence the volume needs* is **this book needs**; **:79** — the closing paragraph's *the reader of this body said out loud in a room with the doors shut* is **given back to the woman who does the sums, said standing up in the ordinary voice in the yard**, which is where Ch 184:51–53 has it and where Ch 187:76 already had it right. **Ch 185:5** — the shaft came off **two months back** and not three; **:26** — *the second in this volume and the last* becomes **the second this season and the last**; **:32** — **who held the book** is corrected from the clerk at the top of the slope to **the licensed man at the head of the shaft**, so that :28, :32 and the clerk's own block at :34 agree, which they must, because this is the volume's second and last use of a rule whose four lines are about a stop entered by whoever holds the book; **:34, :76** — the rule's uses are **this season**; **:74** — *this is the second use of the standing rule in this volume* is **this season**. **Ch 186:21** — the woman stood in that yard at **the fifth hour of the morning** and not the seventh, against the clerk's own entry at :25 and her walking up the road at the fourth; **:37** — **that sheet is nine years** and not eleven, the household having counted for eleven and delivered nine years of the count, which :20, :25 and :29 all say and which :67's *eleven years of counting* is the household and not the sheet; **:45** — *the sentence that is the chapter* is **the sentence the day is for**; **:67** — the fourth cart is **three months back** and not four. **Ch 187:19** — *used twice in this volume* becomes **twice this season**; **:72** — a missing paragraph break and a missing space are restored, the closing scene having been welded to the forty-eighth section-nine note by *is not going to.Underneath*; **:78** — **a man of about thirty-one fetched for the fifteenth time** is **about seventy**, he being fetched for the sixteenth time on day 350 and Ch 196 being right and this line not; **:82** — *a month of a volume* is **a month of this book**.

**Batch 0005, six chapters touched.** **Ch 190:51** — *the one that decides the chapter* becomes **the one that decides the day**. **Ch 191:60** — *used twice in this volume* becomes **used twice this season**, and the sentence is extended so that the read-out and the use are entered as two counts and not one. **Ch 192:33** — *the fourth thing in this volume that this holding's own body has done that it cannot put in a book* becomes **the fourth thing this season**, and the hold, the corridor three hundred yards south of the drawing, and the plan with the hour on it and not torn up are all untouched. **Ch 194:31** — *the second time in this volume that a figure of this holding's has come back on a piece of paper* is **the second time this season**. **Ch 195:75** — *no chapter of this book is going to make one* becomes ***no page of this book is going to make one***, which is both world-referring and truer, the page being one the fen controls. **Ch 196:57** — the beat is *the twenty-first of a month **five** months back* and not six, inside the one sentence that says the day of the beat is not restated in this book; **:59** — *used twice in this volume and not on any other day* becomes **twice this season**, with the sentence extended to name the ninth and the fourth and to end on *not one of the four lines is about either*; **:90** — *said out loud for the second time in this volume* becomes **the second time this season**. **The chapter keeps its three clerk's blocks and the standing count of questions goes off six on to seven.**

**THE ONE CHAPTER CHANGED ON TOP OF THE CLOSE: Ch 156, :19.** *I have given it to two people in nine years* is **restored to nineteen years**, because the review of the close found the chapter contradicting itself — the same man giving nineteen at :19 and nineteen at :29 in the same chapter — and found that the repair had generalized `outline/volume-04.md:67`, which scopes nine to *reading ground* and to nothing else. **Ch 156 is now internally consistent: nineteen for what the man of about thirty-five has refused (:19, :29), nine for what he has read (:21), and eleven at :89, which is the man of about fifty and a different fact. Ch 186:37 was checked against the same question and LEFT STANDING, because the household's eleven years and the sheet's nine years are two different spans and the chapter says so four times.**

**Volume 04 is 141,727 words over five batches (28,501 + 29,253 + 28,472 + 28,226 + 27,275) and the manuscript is 592,519 over 196 chapters.** Batch 0005 is 27,275 over nine chapters and is inside the 27,000–30,000 range. Ch 190 at 3,126 and Ch 196 at 4,438 are declared outside the 2,600–3,120 per-chapter target and the band is not widened to cover them; Ch 196 is the volume's last chapter and carries the standing-figures ledger and every closing beat. The other two exceptions are Ch 157 at 3,193 and Ch 151 at 2,599. **All four are declared and none is hidden. Volume 04 is closed.**

# VOLUME 05 — BATCH 0001, CHAPTERS 197–206, MOVEMENT 1: FOUR BODIES AND ONE NODE

**Ten chapters, days 351–370, the twenty-first through the thirtieth of the twelfth month and the first through the tenth of the first month, and the year turns inside Chapter 204. The volume outline is `outline/volume-05.md`, the governing prompt is `workspace/volume-05/batch-0001/PROMPT.md`, and the next prompt is `workspace/volume-05/batch-0002/PROMPT.md`. The batch crosses one month and year turn and therefore has TWO naming tables, and both are in `state/continuity.md` under *VOLUME 05 — BATCH 0001*.**

- **197 — *The Condition*** — day 351, the twenty-first of the twelfth month, a Sunday. **One sheet comes up the fen road on the ordinary post from the Rootwright Assembly, four clauses, embossed, under the standing delegated authority of the Assembly, and it names no village, no node, no person and no date.** Clause one: a heartroot serving more than one body of households is a shared installation. Clause two: a shared installation may be held only by a person entered on the roll for that district. **Clause three, which is the line that does the work: where a heartroot is shared and no person on the roll holds it, the heartroot is not to be patterned.** Clause four: a body of households may apply for a person to be named, the Assembly will not name one on an application, and an application is not a vacancy to be filled on the ground. Read whole in a yard of about eleven people by the person who keeps the seed house, who says the standing first and says that somebody is going to want to talk about the fourth line and is going to be allowed to. **THE READER OF THIS BODY SAYS THE CLAUSE IS CORRECT AND GIVES THE REASON FIRST, IN FRONT OF ELEVEN PEOPLE, and does not improve it afterwards: a thing four villages have done for eleven years without anybody on a roll holding it is a thing nobody has ever had to write down, and a thing nobody has ever had to write down is a thing nobody can be sent for, and if it goes wrong at the second hour of a morning there is nobody to send.** He also says, standing up, that a professional body has just told four villages that a thing they have done for eleven years is not a thing it recognizes, and that this is better than what this holding has, and that the count of what is better is not going up because it is true and he is tired. **A man of about forty-four who slept in the cart shed the night before is let speak and says the four villages did not apply, do not know the sheet existed until that afternoon, and that nobody in four villages has ever been asked what four villages do.** Standing figures of an ordinary day, unsoftened: the seventh column at thirty-seven and not read today, the twelfth month's compost line falling on the first of next month, a Wednesday, not known and not asked for, the boards at two hundred and eighty-five days, the March's tin at three hundred and thirty-two days, the use log at fifteen with the reason entered, the mark four inches and branching twice with the four accounts of the arm none of which agrees with another, **and the count of people in this fen who can hold a pattern for four at two, both of this holding, with the clause saying one of them is not allowed to, and the roll for the Tarrow district carrying two names of which one is Coll Renner, in Fennmere, and not in this yard.** Two clerk's blocks.

- **198 — *Four Bodies, One Node*** — day 352, the twenty-second, a Monday. **Brenna Oake, of about forty-four, walks four days to the fen gate for Havergate, and Sennik Vaul, of about thirty-one, for Silling, and each says standing up, before anybody puts anything to them, that they speak for a body of households and not for themselves, and the fen enters that the standing-up sentence has now been said five times in this book.** Neither is of this holding. Figures go in: Havergate about six hundred persons and a hundred and forty-four doors with a fire in them, eleven who decide; Silling about two hundred and ten persons counted at a gate in a month two months back, seven who decide; Low Wether about a hundred and twenty people between them; Marden about three hundred and **no person of Marden in the yard.** The four bodies tell the fen what they do, in that order, and it is not softened: the node is under a board on the north kerb, the board has a column and no heading, and the column is read every ninth day by whichever household is holding, and the holding is done by one person at about the second hour of the morning for about two hours, and Havergate has six who can do it and they take it in turns and **the order has never once been written down and every one of the six knows it.** **Sennik Vaul then says the thing the whole of this book has been walking toward: four bodies on one node is not a division and never was, because a pattern is held by one person at a time and there is no arrangement of four bodies on one node that is anything except a rotation, and the four villages have been dividing a thing that cannot be divided and have been getting away with it because a quarter is a quarter and nobody ever wrote the word quarter anywhere. THE WORD `rota` IS SPOKEN OUT LOUD IN THAT YARD, ENTERED BY THE CLERK, and it is the first time the word has been on a page in this book in four years.** Brenna Oake says, unasked, *we did not know it was called anything, we thought we were being fair*, and is told that being fair is not the same as being safe and is not the same as being written down. **Then the arithmetic, done out loud on the boards over the shaft with a lamp, and had three times by three people: a rota of four turns needs four persons, the count of persons in this fen who can do it is two, and both are of this holding.** The man of the north row with the cough, with his back to the wall throughout, says that a rotation of four with two men in it is a man and a relief, and is not thanked and was not asked. At about the seventh hour Sennik Vaul and the man of the north row go down the ladder into the shaft to look at what the boards are holding up, with the reader of this body at the top of the slope and not going down, **the fifth time he has given that reason and the count is entered.** Sennik comes up and says there is a heart in that shaft, that he has seen forty of them and will not say what he has seen and is not going to be asked, that the one at the Four Elbows is bigger by a long way, and that **the four of them have been taking it in turns for eleven years and none of them has ever had to write down what happens if the person on the turn does not turn up.** The reader of this body says the seventh line of the rota is going to be written by somebody who is not him. One clerk's block.

- **199 — *What The Roll Is For*** — day 353, the twenty-third, a Tuesday. **Coll Renner comes up the fen road with the case and a copper-collared living bead, is announced as licensed and as the holder of one of the two names on the Tarrow roll, and is told by the person who keeps the seed house that he has not been thanked once in eleven visits and is not going to be now.** He reads the four clauses standing up and confirms **all four**, and says he is not going to pick at one of them to make the room feel better, and that a licensed man picking at Assembly paper to make a room feel better is the cheapest thing a professional does. **Then the reason the clause exists, in his own words: in eleven years on the roll he has never seen a hold entered on any form in any district, and the reason is that nobody has ever had to write one, and a hold is a thing a man does with his own body while other people work, and it cannot be given to anybody else afterwards, and an entry is a promise that it can be checked by somebody who was not there, and so a hold is uninspectable and a thing that is uninspectable is not a thing an office can send a man to look at. CLAUSE THREE IS NOT A JUDGMENT ABOUT FOUR VILLAGES, IT IS A SENTENCE ABOUT THE FACT THAT NOBODY CAN BE SENT FOR, AND IT IS THE MOST CORRECT THING HE HAS READ ON THE ASSEMBLY'S PAPER IN ELEVEN YEARS.** He lays the bead against a wetted scrap and it reports in four seconds that the seed was grown in a yard with a wall on the north side and a fire in the same building for at least three seasons, **and then says out loud the other half, which is that the same bead cannot tell anybody whether a man is holding a pattern at the second hour of a morning, cannot tell whether the man is tired, cannot tell whether the man went home, and cannot tell whether the man is any good, and that the only instrument he has met that can read that is the man doing it.** He states that neither name on the roll for the district where the Four Elbows stands is the reader of this body, that this is not a coincidence, and that **a standard which could tell a good man from a bad one would not be a standard, it would be a man with a book.** The reader of this body says the clause is correct, that he is not appealing it, that he is not writing and asking who wrote it, and that the answer to a rule which says only a person on the roll may hold is a second person and a third and a fourth and a document about what happens when one of them goes into a hole, and **that four bodies and two men of one holding satisfies clause three on paper with a man asleep in a village three of the four bodies do not live in, and that the clause has a loophole in it shaped exactly like him.** Renner says that is the only kind of anybody in his profession and also the reason he has never got comfortable. **He asks for one thing at about the fifth hour in a yard of about nine, is refused in the yard with a reason that is in the book, and the thing is not named in this book at his request, and the request was granted in full and in public, and nobody said anything about the refusal and the count of not-sayings is one and it is a count of not-sayings and not a count of refusals.** At the gate, Sennik Vaul proposes a man from the Assembly standing on that ground once a season, and is told by the reader of this body that once a season is the wrong frequency and that the cheapest possible answer is a man with a clipboard at the second hour of a morning, which is cheaper than a line in a rota written by the man who is not going to be on the rota. One clerk's block.

- **200 — *Four Columns And No Headings*** — day 356, the twenty-sixth, a Friday. **A four-line return comes up the fen road from the households of Marden, correct, refusing equal turns and proposing that the four bodies be counted and the node divided in proportion to the count, and enclosing the counting paper, which is the second of its kind, the first being lost.** The counting paper has **four columns, one for each village, and NO HEADING ROW**, and the four numbers in the fourth line down are **144, 96, 48 and 210.** Havergate's is dwellings with a door and a fire. Low Wether's is ninety-six hearths, and a hearth is a fire and a fire is a family and a family is not a house, and the woman of Havergate says she has told them that for eleven years and is not going to be the one who says their number is wrong. Silling's is two hundred and ten persons counted at a gate, and it is the oldest of the four and the only one taken by counting heads. **And the third column, and the forty-eight, and nobody in that yard can say what it is.** Sennik Vaul says carefully that a forty-eight is a number he has seen in three other people's hands in eleven years and it is never a count of people, it is a count of something that was wanted, **and the reader of this body does not write that in the book and does not repeat it, and enters the reason: a good guess in a yard becomes a figure in a book inside nine minutes, and that is the rule this holding has lived on for four years and has never had to pay for until a Friday afternoon.** Then the sum, done twice out loud, with three people: of the four numbers two are households, a hundred and forty-four and ninety-six, **two hundred and forty between them**, and two hundred and ten is persons and forty-eight is nothing anybody can say and is therefore nothing. Havergate is a hundred and forty-four out of two hundred and forty, which is three fifths, and three fifths of one thousand five hundred hundredweight a day is **nine hundred**; Low Wether is two fifths, which is **six hundred**; **nine hundred and six hundred is one thousand five hundred exactly**, had three times by three people and it did not move, **and Marden and Silling get nothing, and their share is not a small share, it is the whole difference between a household and a number.** Brenna Oake stops him: *then you have done to Marden this morning exactly what a piece of paper in a bag did to Havergate on Saturday, and you have decided what our number is from outside our own gate, and it took you eleven seconds.* He agrees in the yard in front of eleven people, writes **eleven words** to Marden asking what the forty-eight is and not what it ought to be, reads it out, has it read back, and puts the hour on it. **Then, at about the seventh hour, he walks the nine hundred yards to the well house door with a lamp and reads all seven headings, or rather reads the one heading and looks at the six places where a heading ought to be, and comes back and says it out loud to a yard of about nine: this holding has spent four years writing down every time it did not know something, and it has had six empty columns on its own well house door for four years and has never written down what any of them is for, and he is not going to write them in tonight and is not going to invent a figure for a body of households that has no charter and no standing.** Brenna Oake says it is the same kind of thing as the forty-eight and that the man on the high ground is going to be embarrassed and the fen is going to find out in about a week, and the fen says yes, and that the reason he is not going to help him is that a man who is embarrassed about a number is a man who has just learned what a column is for. The fiftieth note is entered that night, and the man of about fifty asks for his initial against nothing and the count of that request is forty-eight. One clerk's block.

- **201 — *A Line For Stopping*** — day 357, the twenty-seventh, a Saturday. **The seed side goes out in the rain, nine sacks carried by the engineer of record into a cart bed where he was put, not asked and not thanked and not needed, and the cart shed roof goes again over about two feet and the man of about fifty is on it with a board and a piece of tar at about the fourth hour and nobody thanks him.** The man of the north row with the cough has sold seed on that lane for eleven years and has never left a paper of turnip on a gate and never taken one back, and on the Saturday morning he is at the gate with a paper in his coat and the man of about fifty buys one off him and pays him on the gatepost and neither of them says anything about it. **At about the third hour of the afternoon, in the cart shed, on the back of a printed weekly schedule of this holding, THE MAN OF THE NORTH ROW WITH THE COUGH DRAWS A ROTA, and nobody asked him to and he did not offer and he is not thanked, and the reader of this body comes to the door, finds eight lines already on the paper, is told to write down that the fourth village is in the wrong place and not to say it out loud yet, sits down on an upturned pail and says nothing for about twenty minutes while the eleventh line goes on.** The rota has **eleven lines, four of them turns and seven of them stops.** The turns name the hour, the number of workers and the number of persons who can do it, and the third line says the number at Marden is **NOT COUNTED** and the fourth says Silling has one and he is seventy-one. The stops: a turn does not begin until the holder is on the ground and has said the word, and a turn that has not begun is not a late turn; a turn that has not happened is taken by the next body on the list; **the seventh stop line is that a holder who is asleep and cannot be woken is neither, and the next body is woken, and then the one after, and if neither can be woken the turn is not taken, the fields wait, and the fields waiting is entered by whoever enters anything with the day and the hour on it, and nobody is blamed for a field that waited**; nobody holds a turn for a body that has not asked; a body may stop any turn of its own at any time without saying why and without saying why before; the holder stops on one word, the same word, said out loud so that somebody hears it; and the eleventh line says it is a draft and is not in force because three of the four bodies have not seen it and two have not answered. **THE SEVENTH LINE IS EXPLAINED FROM NINE YEARS OF THE MAN'S OWN LIFE, and he says it to the door and not to anybody: he kept a turn for nine years, eleven miles off, at a well, for a man who was on his own and who died in the middle of a Tuesday, there was nothing written down, he went up at the second hour the next morning and did not know whether he was allowed to, and he did it for six more days because nobody told him to stop, and in those six days he put water on a thing he was not certain he had standing to water, and he has never found out since what it was. THAT IS WHAT A SCHEDULE NOBODY HAS RUN IN A BAD HOUR IS. A SCHEDULE IS A PIECE OF PAPER SOMEBODY HAS ALREADY OBEYED ONCE IN THE WORST HOUR THEY ARE GOING TO HAVE THIS SEASON, AND THE SEVENTH LINE IS THE LINE THAT HAS BEEN OBEYED. EVERY OTHER LINE IN THAT IS A HOPE.** Then he says why he wrote it when he was not asked: he is not going to be on the rota, he is sixty-eight with a cough in the winter, he said yes once for an hour and a quarter and could not stand up straight for two days after and nobody said anything about that either, **and a rota is not a favour to the man who is leaving and not a favour to the man who is staying; it is a thing four villages do between them, and the only people in this fen who could write it are two men who are going to be on it.** The reader of this body says he is not going to touch it and is not going to read it out in a yard that afternoon, and that **the sentence a man who is the only person who can do a thing is a bottleneck and not a system has just been answered by a piece of paper, and the paper is better than the sentence, and the sentence is not coming down and he is not going to say it a third time this month**, and that he has been the only one for two years and has never once been able to say that in a yard without it sounding like a man asking for something. The person who keeps the seed house reads the sheet standing up and does not comment. **The sheet goes up the cart road to three of the four bodies on the Saturday post, and the copy for Silling goes into the bag with the reader of this body's own hand, and the fen enters that he did that with his own hand and that it is not a thing to be entered and is entered.** At the gate that night the man of the north row with the cough says nobody has ever read that out loud in a yard and in about four villages in about a month people are going to read it and think it is his, and he is not going to correct them, and he would like it written down that he said it there and not there. One clerk's block.

- **202 — *The Second Body*** — day 358, the twenty-eighth, a Sunday, in a yard of about eleven. **Tova Reed comes up the fen road with two seed cases and a roll of paper and is announced, and says the standing-up sentence herself before the person who keeps the seed house can say it, and gives the figure: SHE IS OUT ON THE SEED-ROUTE ELEVEN DAYS IN EVERY SEASON OF TWENTY, SHE GOES OUT ON THE SECOND AND IS BACK ON THE THIRTEENTH, AND IT DOES NOT MOVE FOR ANYBODY AND IT IS NOT GOING TO MOVE FOR A ROTA, and it is the first time in this book's history that the eleven has been on a page.** The reader of this body asks her, in the yard, in daylight, in front of the people it is about, to be the second body on the rota, **and gives the reason for asking before he asks it, which is the fourth time he has done that in four years, and the reason is arithmetic: a rota of four turns needs four people, we have two, the count would go from two to three, and three is not four and three is better than two, and he says out loud that the reason is a bad reason and that he is saying it in a yard instead of asking in a room with the doors shut, and does not say *and it would help*, and enters that he decided not to say it before the yard rather than in it.** **She says no, fast, before anybody has finished standing about, without sitting down and without softening it, having said the reason for refusing before anybody asked her for one.** Her reasons, in her own words: she is a day's walk and better from those boards on the days she is out, **and the second body on a rota is not the first body with a smaller job, it is the one everybody leans on when the first is asleep, and a person who can be got is a person who gets got every season for as long as there is anybody to get, and a thing leaned on from a day's walk off does not get leaned on less, it cannot send anybody back, and in about four years it will be a line in a rota that does not fail and cannot be proved to be failing.** Then the harder half, unasked, looking at him and not at the yard: **she is not refusing because she cannot hold a pattern, she has not tried, she is not going to try in a yard or on a rota or at the second hour of a morning with four people working while eleven people watch, and she has a trade, and she can read a line of seed in the ground and she cannot read a thing that is not finished growing, and that is not a smaller thing and is the thing she is for.** She names the cost on his side without being asked: he has held it twice and it has cost him a day and a night each time and a hand for a day and once a headache from the third hour of the afternoon until the fourth the next morning, all of it in a book with the hour on it, and **he has never once said in a yard that he would rather it was somebody else, and she has never once asked him why, and they have both been extremely careful about that for four years.** He says it is no, and that he will not ask her twice, and will not ask her about it in a room with the doors shut, and will not write and ask anybody else in that yard what she would have said yes to. Nobody said anything for about a minute and a half. **Two clerk's blocks: one for the refusal, and one for the counts, in which the man of about fifty says out loud the thing nobody had said, which is that it is not the fen that is short, it is that the fen is two and the rota is four and the only two are of one holding, and a holding with no charter has no chair on a rota drawn up by four other bodies of households, and the fen enters that nobody said that out loud before the man of about fifty said it and that he had not worked out it was true until about four minutes before he said it.** The seedwright sleeps in the cart shed and leaves at the fifth hour on a cart that was already going, and nobody saw her off and the fen entered the reason, which is that a man who stands at a gate to watch a woman leave is a man who has made the leaving into something.

- **203 — *The One Who Came Up The Road*** — day 359, the twenty-ninth, a Monday. The cart shed roof is tarred and holds. **At about the sixth hour of the evening three people come up the fen road on foot in the rain with no cart, having walked since the second hour, and are announced before any of them speaks. SOREN RILL, of about thirty-five, of an Assembly in the Thornwild, states in the standing form that he is not a guide, holds no charter, is on no roll, is not a representative of a body of households, and did not come to be asked anything, and says the same sentence he said in a yard on a road eleven miles south about a month ago and that the fen enters as the same sentence.** The two with him counted the standing willow on the ground where the Four Elbows stands in the last week of a month three months back and give the number from their own paper: **two hundred and thirty-one, of which a hundred and forty-one are along the west side and about ninety along the north, the west worse than the north and the north worse than the middle**, and the woman of about twenty-nine says the middle is the only part anybody looks at and they did not decide to look at the middle, the middle is where a man stands when he is being shown a thing, and nobody thanked her. **Soren Rill says the one thing only he can say about that ground, and it is that the kerb is in four parts and you can read it in the stone, four rebuilds in eleven years, one by each body of households in the order the kerb goes round, a different mason's mark in each, and the third part is not the same stone as the other three because the third part was rebuilt in the fourth year and there was money in that year and there was not in the others — and he says he has had it for nineteen years and nobody has ever come and asked him for it, and he would like it written down that the first time anybody came for it, it was a fen, and it cost two of them a day of rain each way and one of them slept in his shed without being asked, which he did not mention to anybody because mentioning it would have made it a favour.** Then he refuses two things in a yard of about nine in the rain and neither is improved on. One, that he will not be on a rota of four villages' water in a turn, as a relief, as a body who is called, or as a name in the margin, **because he would be there as a favour granted by a person, and the day the person is not in the room the favour is not in the room, and a rota with a line in it for somebody given it out of goodwill is a rota that will be quietly shortened in about four years without anything ever being in writing, and a village on the west side of that node has already lost the water once.** Two, that he will not say who speaks for the ground the kerb is built on and will not say what that ground is doing under the kerb. **His reason, given in that order, is standing and not trust: eleven acres of standing willow with a heart in the middle of them is not the land of four bodies of households and never was, and four villages have rebuilt a kerb around it for eleven years without once asking what the kerb is for, and he will not teach a body of four villages a standing by standing in a yard and being useful at it, because that is how a thing gets taught and that is how it gets lost, and in about nine years the man who taught it will be dead and there will be four villages with a habit and no argument for it, and a habit with no argument is a charter with the reasons taken off the front.** Then, unasked, at about the seventh hour: **there is a fifth party at that node, he will not say who, he has known it for longer than this batch has been running, and he wants that entered exactly as he says it and not improved, because the improvement is the whole of what he is asking not to be done — and that a fifth party at a node is not a fifth claim, and is usually a piece of paper nobody has traced, and is usually somebody doing the work because nobody else was going to, and is usually right, and he is telling the fen that before the fen finds it so that the fen does not have to pretend not to know.** The reader of this body asks ONE question, gives the reason first, does not ask the fifth party again in a yard or in a room, and does not write and ask: **is there a return, not a turn. Does anybody put anything back into that ground, is it written down, and is it in a book anybody but the man who did it can read.** The answer is that there is one person, every ninth day, written on a board at the node, and the board has a column and the column has no heading, and the man who writes on it is not one of the four villages and has not been for about eleven years, and Soren Rill stops there because the fen did not ask him the other question. He says it is the most he has said to a fen in four years and it is because the fen did not ask, and **he is not going to be thanked for a method.** At the gate on the way out he says one thing to the reader of this body alone: that the fen is going to walk into that on the ninth with five people and a great deal of arithmetic, and that there is going to be a man standing there in the dark who has been doing a thing nobody asked him to do, and he wants it remembered in about four years when somebody says the fen should have known. One clerk's block.

- **204 — *The First Of The First*** — days 360 and 361, the thirtieth of the twelfth month and the first of the first month, a Tuesday and a Wednesday, **and the year turns overnight inside this chapter.** On the thirtieth the door is read at about the tenth hour at nine hundred yards off in a full yard of about twenty-five, by the steward, with the reader of this body on one side of her and the man of about fifty on the other, **and the seventh column goes to thirty-eight, and the day-minus figures are read out loud by the clerk before either number, being two hundred and ninety-four days and three hundred and forty-one days, and the drawer is not opened, and nobody thanks anybody and the count of requests against nothing is forty-nine.** On the first, at about the eleventh hour, in a yard of about twenty-five, the standing figures are read with nothing softened, **and the compost line comes first, because on the first of a month it has come first since the beginning of this book, and the word before the figure is EMPTIER, and it is not the word that has been said for three months.** The woman who does the sums says she said *crowded* for the ninth, tenth and eleventh and meant it every time, that she has been standing in that yard for a minute deciding whether to say the second half out loud and is going to, **because the only reason this holding keeps a book anybody may read is so that a person does not get to keep the comfortable half** — and then the whole of it: the figure is going to be eighty pounds, the same figure, the word and the figure have come apart for the first time in four years, and the fen said in this yard about a month ago that a figure which repeats is a worse kind of figure than a figure which moves, and she does not know which of the two is telling the truth, and the page is correct either way, **and she does not know whether the heap is emptier or whether she is, because a heap fed the same is a heap being eaten faster, and she has been the one eating it for four years at a rate this holding has never once written down, and this holding can check that and has not.** The compost entry: **four hundred hundredweight short at four shillings, one thousand six hundred shillings, EIGHTY POUNDS, derived twice out loud with the two agreeing, once forward in shillings and once as forty-eight pence the hundredweight being nineteen thousand two hundred pence being two hundred and forty pence eighty times over, the ninth, tenth and eleventh months also eighty pounds, all four on the same line, THE RUN AT TWELVE MONTHS NOT DISCHARGED IN TWELVE, the costs of the month on four separate lines and not folded into the shortfall, the first month's line falling on the first of next month and not known, the day entered and the figure not, and the day-and-not-the-figure count at three and not reduced.** Then the rest of the standing list in the same yard: **the leak at three-quarters of an inch, not measured, on a THIRD
